Eclipse 17.0 Automatic Deformation

Abstract
This Eclipse 17.0 Automatic Deformation Registration Internal Training Procedure is an educational aid for Varian treatment planning
software.

HIPAA
Varian’s products and services are specifically designed to include features that help our customers comply with the Health Insurance
Portability and Accountability Act of 1996 (HIPAA). The ARIA and VARiS Vision systems use a secure login process, requiring a user
name and password that supports role-based access. Users are assigned to groups, each with certain access rights, which may include
the ability to edit and add data or may limit access to data. When a user adds or modifies data within the database, a record is
maintained of the data that was changed, the users ID and the date and time the changes were made. This establishes an audit trail that
can be examined by authorized system administrators.

Eclipse Treatment Planning System Fair Balance Safety Statement

Intended Use Summary
The Eclipse Treatment Planning System (Eclipse TPS) is used to plan radiotherapy treatments for patients
with malignant or benign diseases. Eclipse TPS is used to plan external beam irradiation with photon, electron
and proton beams, as well as for brachytherapy treatments. In addition, the Eclipse Proton Eye algorithm is
specifically indicated for planning proton treatment of neoplasms of the eye. Eclipse should only be used by
qualified medical professionals.
Important Safety Information
Radiation treatments may cause side effects, which, in some cases, may be serious. Severity can vary
depending on the part of the body being treated. Side effects are related to the type of treatments delivered
and should be discussed between the clinician and the patient.
Medical Advice Disclaimer
Varian as a medical device manufacturer cannot and does not recommend specific treatment approaches.
Individual treatment results may vary.

Automatic Deformable Registration
To perform a deformable registration, a rigid registration must already exist between two image sets.

1. For this patient we can see that the clinic performed 6 CBCTs during the course of this patient’s
treatment.  We will see that the patient’s anatomy will change substantially during the course of this
treatment.
2. Double-click the Original SIM CT, in order to open it

Note:  Automatic Deformable Registration allows the user to automatically deform one
image set to the shape of another.  This allows clinicians to evaluate changes to the
patient’s anatomy, and structures of interest over the course of treatment.  Clinicians can
see and evaluate shrinkage of tumors that have occurred during treatment.
This tool can be used to make decisions regarding the need to change a patient’s treatment
plan. This evaluation may indicate that the patient’s anatomy has changed enough, that it
requires the patient be re-scanned and re-planned.
It is also possible to copy and paste structures from a deformed image to the registered
image.  This can be very useful when looking at statistical changes with structures over the
course of treatment.

1
2

<!-- page 13 -->

3. To begin our rigid Registration, clickthe Registration menu on the tool bar
4. Select Auto Matching

3
4

<!-- page 14 -->

5. For this example, we want to deform the Original Sim to the first CBCT, which is dated 7/26/2011
6. The Source should be the Original Sim.
7. The 7/26/2011 CBCT, should be the Target
8. Click OK

Note:  The Source image will deform to the Target image that is selected during the Rigid
Registration.
Therefore, it is important to set up the Source and Target correctly during the Rigid
Registration, as the Deformable Registration is dependent upon this configuration.

6
7
8

<!-- page 15 -->

9. Complete the automatic rigid registration (not shown)
10. After the rigid registration is complete, you can perform the Deformable Registration
11. From the Registration menu, select Automatic Deformable Registration

11

<!-- page 16 -->

12. Adjust the Region of Interest to include the areas you want to deform.  In this case, be sure to
extend the ROI to cover all of the CBCT.  (The CBCT is the smaller image)
13. When you are done adjusting your ROI (not shown), click Start

Important Note: Any part of the Source image that is outside of the Region of Interest
(ROI), will not be deformed.  Therefore, be sure to include all of the areas (in all 3 planes)
that you will need for evaluating.
Deformation of the Source image will not occur in areas beyond the borders of the Target
image. (For example, the target in this case is the CBCT, which is a shorter scan than the
original CT)

15. Notice the arrow in the registrations that indicates the direction of Source to Target.  The arrow will

16. The Original CT icon has a wavy grid inside the icon, indicating that it has been deformed.

Note:  The deformed CT is only visible when the Deformable Registration is active.   To
activate a Deformable Registration, double-click the wavy line of the Deformable
Registration.
The deformed CT is available for viewing in the Registration and Contouring workspaces
only. It is only available for viewing when the Deformable Registration is active.

case, RMC on PTV2_7

Analyzing Structure Changes Using Statistics
The Statistics tool is very useful for analyzing changes to structures over the course of treatment.  For best
results, the deformed structures should be copied on to the target CT.  (Which you just completed)
To access the Statistics tool, you must activate the Source CT. In this case, this is our Original SIM CT.
1. To activate, double-click the Original SIM CT
2. The Original SIM CT is now active

3. Notice the deformable registrations for all the CBCTs
4. To use the Statistics tool, click the Statistics icon in the tool bar

<!-- page 26 -->

13. From the Table, we can see a lot of statistical information about the structures that we selected.
14. For instance, we can see that the volume of the Left Parotid dropped from an original volume of 22.6
cc, to final volume of 12.7cc
15. The PTV2 volume dropped from an original volume of 229.9 cc, to a final volume of 174.9 cc
16. We can also see Center of Mass shifts in x, y and z directions, plus the Dice Similarity Coefficient

Note:  The Dice Similarity Coefficient is a common statistical value used to define the
similarity of two samples.  A value of 1.0 for example, would indicate the shapes are
identical.  As the Coefficient drops below 1.0, it indicates that the shapes are less alike.

Viewing and Analyzing the Deformation of the CT

There are several tools and methods for evaluating overall changes that occurred in a single deformation.

1. To view and analyze a deformable registration, activate the Deformable Registration by double
clicking on the wavy line for that deformable registration.  For this example, activate the final
deformable registration for this patient.
2. Select the Split Window tool from the tool bar. (The Moving Window is another option)
3. We can use the Split Window to see that there has been a lot of change in the area of  the PTV

1
2
3

<!-- page 29 -->

4. Click the Deformation Info tool
5. Hover over the patient anatomy with the mouse, and the tool tip will follow
6. The tool tip gives the distance that the pixel in the deformed image has changed.
Distance is defined as the total 3D distance of change that occurred at that pixel.  The individual 2D
vectors for displacement in the X, Y and Z planes are also shown in the tool tip.

4
5, 6

<!-- page 30 -->

More about the Deformation Tool
The Deformation Info tool can be used to measure various numerical properties of a deformable registration
at different points in space.  All metrics, including Distance, X, Y, Z components, Divergence and Curl are
calculated on a displacement field.  (The Jacobian determinant is not calculated on a displacement field).

The displacement field describes the shifts that are necessary to map voxels from the frame of reference of
the target image to the frame of reference of the source image.

Important Note: All vectors in the displacement field are pointing from positions of the
target image to positions of the source image.

Distance
The distance metric measures the length of a vector of the displacement field at a given point in space. It
describes the amount of shift in millimeters that has to be applied on a target image voxel to match the
corresponding source image voxel.
X-, Y-, and Z-Component
These metrics display the individual components of the displacement vectors. Because the displacements are
applied after the rigid registration, the x-, y-, and z-components correspond to the shifts along the x-, y-, and
z- axes of the frame of reference of the source image.

<!-- page 31 -->

7. We continue with the Deformation Info tool
8. The Deformation Info tool provides a color background that is scaled from the greatest amount of
deformation to the least.  The color scale appears in the contrast slider bar on the right side.
9. The color slider can be adjusted to turn everything red, that deformed at least a particular distance.
For this example, the slider has been changed to show everything as red that deformed more than 20
mm.

Note:  Deformation values sometimes appear as though they are in air. This is because the
Source image originally occupied that space. The patient’s anatomy has shrunk since the
original (Source) image was taken, such that the patient currently has no tissue in that
space, as is shown in this example.

7
8
9

10. To select other options for the Deformation Tool, click the drop-down menu
11. We can see other options for:
 Divergence
 Curl
 Jacobian Determinant

Important Note: All vectors in the displacement field are pointing from positions of the
target image to positions of the source image.

Important Note: When evaluating the deformed structures, be sure to adjust your blending
slider in the registration to show only the target (CBCT). This allows you to look at the
deformed structures overlaying the CBCT anatomy, so you can evaluate whether the
deformation is accurate.
If you see that the deformation needs editing, you can manually adjust the structure using
the contouring tools.

10
11

<!-- page 33 -->

Divergence
12. Divergence gives us values in the tool tip, as we move the mouse around on the CT.
13. Divergence values can be used to evaluate the displacement field. It appears as a color-coded map
overlaid on the input images, as well as being shown in the tool tip values.
14. Divergence values indicate sinks and sources. Sinks of the displacement field are indicated by a
negative divergence value.  Sources are highlighted with positive divergence value.
15. For this example, the Divergence value is positive 1.33, indicating a source rather than a sink.
16. X, Y, and Z component vector values are also included in the tool tip.

12, 15
16

<!-- page 34 -->

Curl
 Curl detects deformation vector field rate of rotation (vortexes). The operator measures changes
in directions between two neighboring vectors in the displacement field.
 If the displacement field is smooth, changes between neighboring vectors are minimal and CURL
values are low (shown in blue)
 If the displacement field is fragmented with neighboring vectors mapping in different directions,
values of the CURL operator are high (shown in red)

<!-- page 35 -->

Color Overlay
The color overlay assists in displaying areas of Curl, Divergence and Jacobian Determinant in context of
the patient’s anatomy.
Manipulate the scale (on the right-hand side), to show significant values of selected metric (For example,
set the scale to 0<x<1 for Jacobian determinant to show areas of expansion)
This can demonstrate location of critical points (curl, divergence) and expansions and shrinkage of tissue
(Jacobian determinant) with respect to patient anatomy and help in evaluating the deformation vector fields
Examples of Sink, Source and Vortex
17. Divergence Sink
18. Divergence Source
19. Curl

17
18
19

<!-- page 36 -->

Jacobian Determinant
The Jacobian Determinant shows contractions, expansions and folds of the images. (Changes in voxel size)

This metric calculates the determinant of the Jacobian of the coordinate transformation that maps positions
from the target frame of reference to the source frame of reference. The value of the Jacobian determinant
represents the change of volume between the source and the target image at a given point in space.

Values above one denote volume shrinkage of the source image with respect to the target image whereas
values between zero and one denote volume growth.  A Jacobian determinant below or equal to zero detects
local inconsistencies in the vector field such as self-intersections that do not represent correct physiological
motion.

20. Jacobian Scores

 A Jacobian score of 1 indicates no change.
 A Jacobian score of less than 0 indicates a fold over which is undesirable and may indicate that
the deformable registration may not be accurate.
 A Jacobian score between 0 and 1 indicate shrinkage.
 A Jacobian score greater than 1 indicates expansion.

20

<!-- page 37 -->

Deformation Grid
21. Select the Deformation Grid tool
22. The Deformation Grid overlays the patient
23. Distortions in the gird indicate where deformation has taken place.  The more the gird is distorted, the
more deformation has taken place.
24. This can be used in conjunction with other evaluation tools like the Deformation Info tool (not shown)

21
22
23
23

<!-- page 38 -->

Target Registration Error
Image Registration now gives the user the ability to conduct landmark based analysis of deformable and rigid
registrations. This is a quantitative technique that assesses the accuracy of the registrations within a volume
of interest and max errors.
Target Registration Error is the distance between corresponding points defined on registered images after a
registration has been applied.
Typically, a few patient cases would be selected for the purpose of Quality Assurance.
You can create a new registration or select an existing registration.
25. From the Registration menu, select Target Registration Error.

26. The Target Registration Error window opens.  The registered images appear.

25

<!-- page 39 -->

27. Place a minimum of 3 points on both images (Just like you would, if you were performing a point
match when creating a rigid registration.  Except, in this case you already have a registration, and you
are manually selecting points to determine the error that might exist in your existing registration)

28. To add additional points, RMC inside the window and select Add Point Pair

<!-- page 40 -->

29. As you place points on the images, the table on the right side shows the Error Statistics regarding
your point placement.

Prescribe Treatment
Prescribe Treatment is an ARIA workspace used to enter a Radiotherapy Prescription for the patient. The
workspace allows for keeping an electronic history of prescriptions for each course of Radiotherapy and any
revisions to those prescriptions. The tabs used to navigate the workspace are:
 Manage Prescription
 New Templates
 Manage List Values

Note: Access to the Prescribe Treatment workspace is rights driven. The User must have
appropriate rights assigned in VSP> Sub-tab: Security, Sub-tab: Rights.

There are 5 Users Rights that affect the Prescribe Treatment workspace.

1) Customize look up values for RT Prescription:  Allows the user to Manage List Values
2) Review Prescription:  Allows user to change status of prescription to Reviewed Prescription
3) Approve an RT Prescription:  Allows user to Approve an RT Prescription
4) Read Prescription: Allows user to read prescription
5) Editing Prescription:  Allows user to edit a prescription.  This right is necessary for creating a new
prescription as well as editing existing prescriptions

<!-- page 28 -->

Finalizing the Prescription
The Clinician has several options for finalizing the prescription.
1) These options for saving the prescription are:
2) Save as Draft – Using this option allows the Clinician to return to the prescription and edit it
without having to error the original prescription.
3) Approve – This option allows the Clinician to finalize the prescription. An approved prescription
can still be edited, but the system will retire the original prescription and save the edits as a new
prescription.
4) Review Prescription – Allows the Clinician who entered the prescription to “sign off” on their work
for audit purposes but leaves the approval to another authorized Clinician.
5) Error Out – Allows the clinician to Error Out a previously saved Prescription.
6) Save as Template – Using this allows the clinician to use the current prescription as a template for
future cases with a similar diagnosis and treatment plan. Templates can be Private or Shared.
Private Templates are accessible only by the Clinician who entered the template into the system.
Shared Templates are available to all staff Clinicians to use.
7) Cancel – This option allows the user to clear their work and cancel out of the entry mode.

Clinical Example:  A clinician might choose Reviewed Prescription in a medical school
environment when a physician or resident might change the status of the prescription to
Reviewed Prescription to indicate that it has been reviewed.  Another physician might
later Approve the prescription.

10 - Link RT Prescription to Existing Plan

It is possible to Link and Unlink an RT Prescription to an existing plan. For this example, we are going to
unlink the RT Prescription from the plan we just created. Then we are going to use the unlinked plan to
demonstrate how to link it to the prescription.

1) Notice how the plan is nested below the prescription. This indicates that the plan is linked to that
prescription.

6) Click on the white space (No prescription selected)

<!-- page 57 -->

11) Notice that the plan is no longer nested under the prescription. If a plan is not nested under a
prescription that indicates that it is not linked to any prescription.

Note:  Linking a plan to a prescription is optional. The plan can be approved and treated
without being linked to a prescription. In fact, using RT Prescriptions is entirely optional.
There is no requirement that an RT Prescription be created or linked to a plan.

Note:  Linking a plan to prescription does enable certain functionality. The prescription is
generally in control of the dose and number of fractions of the linked plan. (However, this
can be over-written after the prescription is linked to the plan, although this is not
recommended).
If a prescription that is attached to a plan is revised, then the linked plan will be changed to
Planning Approved status, (if it is already Treatment Approved), to prevent it from being
treated. This is a safety feature designed to encourage a plan revision in accordance with
the prescription revision, before the plan is treated again.
We will demonstrate this feature later in this procedure.

41) The Linking Plan to Prescription window appears.
42) This window forces the planner to decide how to reconcile the dose and number of fractions from the
plan with the prescription revision.

Note:  If the planner chooses to subtract the treated sessions from the number of fractions
in the prescription, then the plan will have fewer fractions left to treat than what is shown in
the prescription.

Important Note: Then planner needs to be sure they understand the physician’s intent.
This is one reason it is good practice for the physician to include notes with their
prescription revision. (When they edit the original prescription)

13 - Plan Parameters Overview
Plan Parameters workspace gives an overview of the treatment parameters of a patient’s Treatment
Courses, Plans and related fields. From here a user can identify parameters associated to fields such as
field names, mu’s, field size, gantry and collimator angles, SSD, Machine Energy, dose rate, DRR’s, etc.
This workspace can also be used to modify some parameters within a plan, or to create a simple new
photon plan or electron plan. However, most planning should be done in a treatment planning application
such as Eclipse.

Important Note for Eclipse Users: If you are doing all treatment planning in Eclipse, there
will not be any reason to use the Plan Parameters workspace.
Plan Parameters can be used for creating 2D plans and electron plans if you are planning
without a CT Sim, or if you do not plan to calculate the dose.
Plan Parameters is also useful for therapists who wish to quickly review parameters for all
fields within a plan.
Plan Parameters is one of the RTM workspaces, therefore, it is important to understand
the overview.
Plan Parameters Workspace Overview
Navigate to Plan Parameters workspace using QuickLinks drop-down.  For this example, open patient
Jennifer Gilliano’s plans.
1) To easily see the Courses and Plans associated with the patient, it is helpful to open the Context
Window, if it is not already open. Click on the Context Window icon.
2) Also, for easier navigation through the RTM workspaces, click on the Workspace Bar.

4) The Scope pane (Upper section) gives an outline of the Courses and Plans.

Plan Icons:
This section explains the various icons of Plans and their related status. When a plan is created, it is
Unapproved by default. It is usually Planning Approved, prior to being Treatment Approved.
1) A plan is capable of being treated on a treatment machine once it is Treatment Approved. These
two levels of approval help to ensure that the plan has been thoroughly reviewed prior to treatment
by the Dosimetrist, Physician, and Physicist, depending on a department’s workflow.

Note: A plan must be Treatment Approved in order to be available for treatment. (In a
totally Varian environment). Treatment Approved plans will have a green outline around
the plan icon.

2) Unapproved plan icon (no outline). Plan can still be edited and is not ready for treatment
3) Planning Approved icon (has Blue outline) Plan approved by qualified specialist and is ready for
treatment approved
4) Treatment Approved icon (has Green outline) Plan is ready for treatment
5) Retired Plan icon (Grayed-out) Original plan is changed to retired when treated plan is modified.
No changes can be made to a retired plan.
6)  Rejected Plan icon (strike through) Plan has been created as a potential candidate but rejected
for actual patient treatment.

Activating the Advanced Feature Set:
1) The Advanced Feature Set must be turned on for a user to be able to edit many of the non-
dosimetric parameters for a Field within Plan Parameters.
2) To turn on the Advanced Feature Set (you must be in the Plan Parameters workspace)
3) Select Tools dropdown.
4) Select Task Configuration.

Note: With the Advanced Feature Set activated, the user can set images as reference
images, create and edit layers, and modify non-dosimetric data.
The Advanced Feature Set is specific to the Logged in User. Once the Advanced Feature
Set is activated for the logged in user, it will stay active unless the user deactivates it.

Note: When the Advanced Feature set is turned on in the Plan Parameters workspace a
user can edit the following non-dosimetric parameters in a Treatment Approved Plan:
 Couch Lng, Lat, and Vrt;
 Add, delete and edit setup Notes and Field Photos.
Imager positions are not editable when plan is Treatment Approved.

14 - Reference Points
Beginning with version15.5, Reference Points now has a dedicated workspace and is no longer
embedded in the Plan Scheduling workspace.
1) It is available under QuickLinks within the Treatment Management category as well as through
the RTM Workspace Bar.

 Reference points can still be created, edited and added to plans in the Plan Parameters
workspace.
 Review of total plan doses can be done here.
 Dose limits and breakpoints can be edited and reviewed here.
 Reference points without location (DPV) can be added here in Reference Points, or External
Beam Planning.
 Reference points with location should be added to the patient’s plan in External Beam Planning.

1

<!-- page 96 -->

To manage Reference points, Click on the Reference Points tab.

4) You may filter to view Primary, Secondary and reference points Without Dose.
5) The green dot indicates this point is the Primary Reference Point.

Note: Since this reference point does not have a physical location, it is considered to be a
DPV (Dose Prescription Volume).
A DPV merely tracks the prescribed dose. (Actual dose can only be tracked to a reference
point with location).

13) Enter Dose Limits for Total, Daily and Session

Note: If you were going to be treating a patient twice per day (BID), you would need to
double the Daily Dose Limit (For this example you would need to increase the Daily dose
to R Lung PTV to 400, and Cord Max to 300)

Important Note:  Incorrect Dose Limits can lead to patient mistreatment. Dose Limits are
provided for patient safety and prevent overtreatment. The Dose Limits are simultaneously
monitoring Total Dose, Daily Dose and Session Dose to the primary reference point. If any
Dose Limit is exceeded, ARIA will prevent the therapist from treating the patient.

13

<!-- page 101 -->

Caution:  ARIA verifies the dose limits before each treatment. If the dose will be exceeded
during treatment, the 4DITC will display an alert prompting the therapist to override dose
before continuing treatment. The therapist must have the necessary rights to override these
dose values.
Dose limits are provided for patient safety and should not be over-ridden without approval of
physics and administration and under the direction of a physician.

14) The Delivered Dose in Course, Remaining Planned Dose and Dose to be Recorded, will begin
to populate with values as the plan is treated and dose is automatically recorded back to the
reference point.

15 - Treatment Preparation
Treatment Preparation is used to manage treatment plans in order to prepare them for treatment.
1) Areas of the workspace include:
 Patient Summary
 Treatment Setup
 Validation and Approval
 Field Parameters


Note to Eclipse Users:
The Treatment Preparation workspace may be used as a last step prior to Treatment
Approval. This workspace allows the planner to for check for Warnings or Errors in the
Validation and Approval pane.
This workspace allows the planner (or other user) to easily manage setup notes and setup
photos.

Important Note for Eclipse Users:
If you are creating your Setup fields and DRR’s in External Beam Planning, then you may
be able to bypass the Treatment Preparation workspace and go directly to the Plan
Scheduling workspace. The Plan Scheduling workspace allows the planner to check for
Warnings or Errors in the Validation and Approval pane. The planner can also schedule the
plan and treatment approve it from the Plan Scheduling workspace.


Note to Eclipse Users:
This workspace is very useful when importing plans from third party treatment planning
systems. If the planning CT is imported with the plan, the planner can then create setup
fields, create DRR’s, add layered drawings to the DRRs, and verify all field parameters.

previously noted, changes will apply to all highlighted fields – whether that is a single field or
multiple fields.

not necessary to manage this pane.

Validation and Approval Pane
The Validation and Approval pane within the Treatment Preparation workspace serves as ARIA’s internal
audit system alerting the user to Errors and Warnings regarding the selected treatment plan. Errors are
indicated with a red dot and must be addressed before the plan can be approved for treatment. Warnings,
indicated by a yellow dot, will not prevent approval for treatment, but are things Varian Medical Systems feels
should be reviewed to be consistent with good clinical practice.
52) Warnings or Errors displayed in black text, indicate that they can be resolved on the current
page.
53) Warnings or Errors displayed in blue text indicate that they must be resolved on another page.
The blue text is a hyperlink that will take you to the appropriate page to resolve.

Note: The Validation and Approval pane is also available in the Plan Scheduling
workspace. If the customer is only using Eclipse for Treatment Planning, then it is likely that
they will navigate directly to Plan Scheduling. They may not need to use the Treatment
Preparation workspace.
However, if they are using multiple planning systems and importing plans into ARIA, then
the Treatment Preparation workspace will be a very important tool.

Schedule an Image Only Session

Note: Facilities that “image only” on the patient’s first day of treatment can schedule an
Image Only Session. This will prevent accidental treatment by staff, by locking the treatment
fields on the first day.

47) To schedule an Image Only Session:
48) Enter the number of days to Delay the treatments.

Note: If there is only one plan at present for the Course of treatment, then enter a “1” for the
Delay. If previous plans in the Course have been treated, then you would need to enter an
appropriate number. The Delay counts all treatment sessions in the Course.
If scheduling Delays for plans where treatments in the same Course already exist, care
should be taken to confirm the scheduled delays you enter are performing as Image Only
Sessions as per your expectation at the treatment machine.

49) Click Schedule.
50) An open instance is inserted in front of the scheduled fractions

Entering a Manual Treatment:
You may choose to enter a manual treatment to account for previous treatment(s), that were not recorded into
ARIA for some reason.
For this example, we are going to enter a manual treatment to account for an emergency treatment that was
clinically set up and completed on a whole brain over the weekend, (prior to the plan being created on the
following Monday). Now that the plan is created and Treatment Approved (on Monday), the physicist wants to
enter the manual treatment. Use patient John Nichols for this exercise (C2, Whole Brain).

Copy and Pasting Plans
If you copy and paste a plan, (possibly as a template for a boost plan or to revive a retired plan) the
original plan will remain treatment approved and still be capable of being treated. The new copied plan will
need several edits as shown in the following steps.

Important Note: You may choose to copy a plan, instead of doing a plan revision.
Varian recommends using the plan revision process instead of copying and pasting plans.
Keep in mind a Plan Revision automatically retires the original plan, while retaining the
remaining scheduled fractions, and remaining reference point doses.
If you copy and paste a plan, you will either need to manually manage each of these issues,
or you can use the “Replace Existing Plan” button (shown below in the Plan Scheduling
workspace) to replace an existing plan under treatment with a new plan. This will retire the
existing plan, and retain the current fractionation and reference point dosages, like the
process of a plan revision.

Important Note: If you copy and paste a plan, the original plan will remain Treatment
Approved and will still be capable of being treated. If the original plan is not going to
continue to be treated, it should be changed to a status of Unapproved, Completed early or
Retired, to prevent it from being accidentally treated.

Important Note: You may Copy and Paste plans from within the Eclipse External Beam
Planning workspace as well.
Be sure to follow the process established by your physics department.

Note: If you are copying and pasting a plan that contains a 3D dose calculation, and
will be making dosimetric changes to the plan, Varian recommends that you perform
those changes in Eclipse External Beam Planning.

17 - RT Summary
RT Summary workspace gives a detailed history of the patient’s radiation treatment and imaging.
From RT Summary, a user can review the current and remaining dose and fractions. Approved and
unapproved images in the patient’s record can be quickly seen. Details of historical treatment parameters
of the actual fields treated can be seen here.
Radiation treatment history is View Only here as records cannot be modified in this workspace, except for
Dose Corrections.

Viewing a Patient’s Radiation Treatment History:
1) To view a patient’s radiation treatment history, navigate to RT Summary workspace.
2) The Course Timeline appears at the top of the screen.
3) Choose the treatment Course for which you want to see history.
4) If there is more than 6 months of history for this course, the Display Complete History checkbox
will appear. Select this checkbox.

Note: RT Summary initially loads with about 6 months of history, to save time opening the
patient. The checkbox for Display Complete History gives the user the option of seeing
more data if desired.

that occurred more than 2 years ago, (for this example), and confirms that you want to proceed.

8) The scroll bar allows you to navigate through several months of data.

9) Moving the scroll bar to the left show’s history from a few months earlier.

# Eclipse_17.0_4D_Planning_Internal_Training_Procedure

<!-- page 1 -->

Eclipse 17.0 4D Planning
Procedure

<!-- page 2 -->

Abstract
This Eclipse 17.0 4D Planning Procedure is an educational aid for Varian Eclipse Treatment Planning software.
Notice
Information within this document is subject to change without notice and does not represent a commitment on the part of Varian. Varian
is not liable for errors contained in this document or for incidental or consequential damages in connection with furnishing or use of this
material. This document contains proprietary information protected by copyright. No part of this document may be reproduced, translated,
or transmitted without the express written permission of Varian Medical Systems, Inc.
HIPAA
Varian’s products and services are specifically designed to include features that help our customers comply with the Health Insurance
Portability and Accountability Act of 1996 (HIPAA). The ARIA and VARiS Vision systems use a secure login process, requiring a user
name and password that supports role-based access. Users are assigned to groups, each with certain access rights, which may include
the ability to edit and add data or may limit access to data. When a user adds or modifies data within the database, a record is
maintained of the data that was changed, the users ID and the date and time the changes were made. This establishes an audit trail that
can be examined by authorized system administrators.
Trademarks
Eclipse™ treatment planning system, Acuros® XB, ARIA® oncology information system, Calypso® system, Clinac® iX
linear accelerator, Dynamic Targeting® IGRT technology, Exact® couch, Exact® IGRT couch, On-Board Imager® kV
imaging system, Enhanced Dynamic Wedge™, Smart Segmentation® automatic contouring utility, Trilogy® system,
TrueBeam® STx system, Millennium™ 120-leaf MLC.
All other trademarks or registered trademarks are the property of their respective owners.
© 2020 Varian Medical Systems, Inc.
All rights reserved.

<!-- page 3 -->

Eclipse Fair Balance Safety Statement

Intended Use Summary
The Eclipse Treatment Planning System (Eclipse TPS) is used to plan radiotherapy
treatments for patients with malignant or benign diseases. Eclipse TPS is used to plan
external beam irradiation with photon, electron and proton beams, as well as for internal
brachytherapy treatments. In addition, the
Eclipse Proton Eye algorithm is specifically indicated for planning proton treatment of
neoplasms of the eye. Eclipse should only be used by qualified medical professionals.
Important Safety Information
Radiation treatments may cause side effects, which in some cases, may be serious. Severity can
vary depending on the part of the body being treated. Side effects are related to the type of
treatments delivered and should be discussed between the clinician and the patient.
Medical Advice Disclaimer
Varian as a medical device manufacturer cannot and does not recommend specific treatment
approaches. Individual treatment results may vary.

<!-- page 5 -->

Importing Images for 4D Planning
Section 1: General Information Regarding 4D Planning
 4D planning is an extension of 3D planning.
 4D planning aids the user in visualizing organ motion in order to plan for gated or
non-gated treatment.
 4D planning in Eclipse allows the visualization of motion in all image views during
any planning function.
 Most image studies produced by medical imaging devices (CT scans) are arranged as
a series of 2D digital images.

The images are imported from the imaging device in digital format and are used
to create a 3D model (image) of the patient.

Stacking the imported 2D slices forms the 3D image.
 Motion of the patient during CT imaging can create unwanted motion artifacts that can
change the apparent geometrical shape and/or location of the organs or the target
structure.

4D imaging introduces the additional dimension of time that accommodates
movement during imaging.

With 4D imaging, it is possible to view structure movement.
 In 4D imaging, image data sets are typically obtained at various stages of the respiratory cycle.

The obtained image data is ‘binned’ (organized into separate folders); according to
the stages of the respiratory cycle, using a third-party external program.

The image data is ‘binned’ based on the phase and amplitude of the respiratory cycle.

These ‘binned’ image data sets are then used to create separate 3D image sets
which are combined together to create a 4D image set.

<!-- page 6 -->

 Below is a diagram of a 4D CT Image Acquisition of phased gating and how it is binned
based on different phases of the respiratory cycle. See Figure 1.
 A series of images are taken throughout the entire breathing cycle while the couch is
at one position. Those images are then binned (sorted) according to a predefined
percentage value of the breathing cycle.
 Then the couch is moved to the second position and images throughout the entire
breathing cycle are obtained and binned.
Figure 1: Retrospective 4D CT Image acquisition

<!-- page 7 -->

 The 4D image study is comprised of multiple 3D images. These 3D images may
consist of the following image types:

Free Breathing image (FB) – a conventional CT scan where the patient is
scanned without an attempt to control the patient’s respiratory cycle or correct the
CT scan data for respiration movement.

Phase Image – an image is taken during a particular phase of the respiratory
cycle and labeled with a phase number (for example 50%, 60%). Phase images
can be displayed in a movie loop.

Maximum Intensity Projection image (MIP) – an image containing the
maximum intensity pixels derived from all phases. An MIP image is useful, for
example for showing bones, or a tumor that is of higher density than the
surrounding area.

Average image – an image where the image data values for each pixel on each
slice are averages of all the image values at the same pixel locations on the
corresponding slices in each bin.

Minimum intensity projection image (Min or MinIP) – an image containing low
intensity pixels derived from all phases.

A 4D image can contain only one 3D image of each type (E.g.: MIP, Average
image, MinIP). Phase images are an exception as they can be created for each
phase/bin however; the phase numbers must be unique.

3D images (part of the 4D image) may be viewed in a movie loop or blended
and can also display temporary DRR images for the volume(s) being viewed.
 Beginning with Eclipse Version 13, the system has been able to create MIP (Maximum
Intensity Projection), MinIP (Minimum Intensity Projection) and AveIP (Average
Intensity Projection) from a 4D data set.
 The system has been able, using DICOM Import Export Application, to import into
Eclipse the ‘breathing trace’ and it’s associated ‘waveform’ from the CT scanner.

The breathing trace will be designated with ‘MP’ (Motion Protocol) and the
waveform will be designated with ‘MW’ (Motion Wave) when viewed in the
DICOM Import Workspace.

The system uses the RPM Converter program to convert the breathing trace and
waveform into DICOM Format. This software program is supplied by Varian to
the CT scanner and maybe accessed through the Start menu of the scanner.
The breathing trace and waveform are necessary to be able to view motion in
Eclipse.
 Once imported, the 4D data set may be viewed in the Contouring Workspace. A movie,
Respiratory Amplitude distribution (a ‘box and whiskers’ plot) or 4D volume statistics
may be used to review the motion/change of position of structures within the 4D set.
 By reviewing the breathing trace (Respiratory Amplitude distribution); it may be
possible to determine if the patient is a suitable candidate for a ‘Gating’ technique.
Large distributions in the Respiratory Amplitude distribution within one phase may
indicate an unstable breathing trace. The patient may need breathing coaching and
the scan may need to be repeated; or, the patient may not be a candidate for ‘Gated’
treatment.

<!-- page 8 -->

<!-- page 15 -->

4D Contouring
Section 1: GTV Contouring Approaches
There are several, although similar, approaches to contouring the GTV that
encompasses the motion of the tumor during the breathing cycle; following are three:
Contour the GTV on all phases:
 Contour the GTV on all phases or phases selected for treatment;
 Review 4D statistics and 4D movie to observe the excursion of the tumor;
 Use this information to determine which phases to use for treatment and create
an AveIP from those phases (You need one CT image for planning);
 Accumulate the AveIP (or the 3D image) chosen for treatment.

Contour the GTV on one phase and use ‘Rigid’ or ‘Deformable’
propagation to populate structure(s) to other phases (A Smart
Segmentation license is needed to use ‘Deformable’)
 Contour GTV (and any other structures, such as Body) on one phase;
 Propagate the GTV to all phases using Rigid or Deformable;
 Review the resulting structures on all phases;

Important Note: It is essential to carefully review any propagated
structures and modify them when necessary.

 Review the 4D movie and 4D Statistics to observe the excursion of the GTV to
select the phases for treatment;
 Create an ITV from the iGTV (standard expansion = 8mm margin); review
and make any necessary adjustments;
 Create PTV from ITV (standard expansion = 3mm for CBCT review and 5mm for kV-kV review)

<!-- page 16 -->

Contour the GTV on to the MIP
 Determine the treatment phases by reviewing the 4D movie or by drawing
the GTV on the Minimum (0%) and the Maximum (50%) phases of
Expiration;
 Use the 4D Statistics Tool to review the maximum GTV motion in the X, Y, and Z directions;
 Create a MIP from the phases to be used for treatment; Review the
Respiratory Amplitude for this;
 Contour the GTV (known as the iGTV onto the MIP using the Lung window; check
coverage and adjust as needed;
 Create an AveIP from the treatment phases;
 Copy iGTV to AveIP;
 Create an ITV from the iGTV (standard expansion = 8mm margin); review
and make any necessary adjustments;
 Create PTV from ITV (standard expansion = 3mm for CBCT review and 5mm for kV-kV review);
 Contour any other structures needed for treatment.

<!-- page 17 -->

Section 4: Respiratory Amplitude Distribution
1) To determine the treatment phases to use for Gating treatment, view the Respiratory
Amplitude Distribution.
a) Click 4D menu > Respiratory Amplitude Distribution. See Figure 32.

Figure 32: 4D menu > Respiratory Amplitude Distribution

b) The Respiratory Amplitude Distribution dialog box opens. See Figure 33.

Figure 33: Respiratory Amplitude Distribution

<!-- page 30 -->

Respiratory Amplitude Information
 This graph is known as a ‘box & whiskers’ graph.
 The Pink and Green Lines indicate:

If the distances between the Green lines is small (as in 40-60% ranges), it
indicates that the data is more reproducible; the breathing is more stable.

The Pink lines may be further from the Green lines if during different
breathing cycles, the patient’s breathing amplitude changed.
 Since every person is different there isn’t a ‘normal’ distribution however the
above example indicates that the patient has stable breathing.

The result should look similar to a smile.
 If the graph was flatter or boxes greater, this might indicate that the scan was not
good and may need to be repeated; or it may be the patient’s inability to breathe
regularly.
 It is important to ‘know the patient.’

The patient may need breathing coaching to help stabilize his/her
breathing and the scan may need to be repeated.

Section 3: Plan Uncertainty Evaluation
 General Information: Reference: Eclipse Photon and Electron Instructions for Use P1047678-
001 Rev A Pages 295-298.
“Photon planning contains uncertainty factors caused by, for example, organ motion and
patient setup errors. There may also be variation in CT numbers obtained from the CT
scanner and in their conversion into electron density or mass density.
Using the plan uncertainty tools, you can calculate plan uncertainty doses that can be used
to evaluate the robustness of the plan in External Beam Planning. Plan uncertainty doses
are based on a nominal (original) plan that is varied by a percentage of error in the CT
calibration curve and by shifts of the treatment isocenters in the X, Y and Z directions. The
isocenter shift coordinates are shown in the planning coordinate system selected in RT
Administration. You can create the parameters for varying the dose in the Plan Uncertainty
Parameters dialog box. When creating the parameters, consider the number of dose
variations you want to achieve in regard to the capacity of the system. The more properties
and uncertainty parameters the plan has, the more time and memory the calculation
consumes.
In photon planning, you have two options for evaluating plan uncertainties – Patient
Setup Error and Target Shift. The selected option is applied to all variations of the
plan.
By selecting the Patient Setup Error option, you can estimate the dosimetric effect of a
patient setup error on structures. You can estimate how differences between the planned
patient setup and treated patient setup affect the dose distribution. The presumption in this
option is that all structures move similarly. You can enter several isocenter shift values and
see their effect on the DVH curves. The DVH curve variations are shown for all structures,
including target structures.
By selecting the Target Shift option, you can estimate the dosimetric effect on non-target
structures in case the tumor moves in respect to the rest of the anatomy. This option is
meant for image-guided treatments, during which you can compensate for the target shift
and ensure that the target is aligned with the beam as intended in the plan. That is why the
DVH curve variations are shown only for non-target structures.
Plan uncertainty doses are always associated with the nominal plan. Calculation of plan
uncertainty doses is possible only if the dose of the nominal plan is valid. If the dose of the
nominal plan is invalidated, or if the plan uncertainty parameters of a calculated plan
uncertainty dose are modified, the plan uncertainty dose is invalidated.
Plan uncertainty doses can be calculated at the same time as the nominal dose, or they can
be calculated separately after the dose for the nominal plan has been calculated. In the
Focus window, a calculated
plan uncertainty dose is shown with icon
. Invalid or not calculated plan uncertainty dose is shown
with icon.         The parameters that were used to create the plan uncertainty dose are
shown after the plan uncertainty dose ID in the Focus window. You can evaluate the plan
uncertainty doses with the dose visualization tools and compare the DVH of the nominal
plan with the plan uncertainty doses.

<!-- page 52 -->

Plan uncertainty doses are saved in the database, and you can open and view them later
in External Beam Planning when you open the nominal plan they are associated with. Plan
uncertainty doses are for evaluation purposes only, and you can only export the DVH in
tabular format.
You cannot create plan uncertainty doses for reviewed plans, approved plans, or verification plans.
You can calculate plan uncertainty doses before treatment. If you want to calculate plan
uncertainty doses during the treatment, you can copy the approved plan, and calculate the
uncertainty doses in the copied plan.”

<!-- page 53 -->

Section 6: Evaluate Plan Uncertainty Doses
1) Plan robustness may be evaluated by comparing the nominal plan’s dose to the plan uncertainty doses.

created. It is possible to evaluate where the biggest differences in dose may occur if there are setup
errors or target shifts.

in the plan uncertainty doses. If the plan normalization method of the nominal plan is changed, the plan
normalization value (shown in Plan Properties) ensures that plan uncertainty doses can be compared with
the nominal plan. The change of normalization method is always made in the nominal plan, and after the
change the dose matrices in both plans are scaled by the same plan normalization value.

Section 6: DVH Based Plan Conversion
 General Information Reference: Eclipse Photon and Electron Reference Guide P1047679-001
Rev A Page 406
“The Plan Conversion feature makes changes in the source plan to best replicate
the calculated dose when using another treatment unit. You can also convert a
plan between treatment techniques (for example, convert a VMAT plan to an IMRT
plan).
The tool is trying to reproduce the DVH of the original plan using the selected
treatment unit. After the plan conversion, you need to evaluate and approve the
plan normally.
The following plan conversions are supported:
To/From
Varian VMAT
Varian IMRT
Varian 3DCRT
Varian VMAT
—
Yes
—
Varian IMRT
—
Yes
—
Varian 3DCRT
—
—
Yes
Non-Varian VMAT
—
Yes
—
Non-Varian IMRT
—
Yes
—
Non-Varian 3DCRT
—
—
Yes

The Plan Conversion feature does not support conversion from or to the Halcyon
treatment unit, and it is disabled for Halcyon-based treatment plan.
The plan conversion creates a new plan revision of the source plan and sets it to the
“Unapproved” state. The dose distribution is calculated automatically using fixed MU,
and the normalization mode is set to No Normalization. Any setup fields or scheduled
treatment sessions are deleted. The revision will retain the dose per fraction
information. The comment field of the converted plan contains a reference to the
source plan. The converted plan is deleted if there is an error in the dose calculation,
or if you abort the dose calculation.
The original plan is not touched; it will retain the original plan information.
The following preconditions apply for the plan and are checked by Eclipse when you
convert a plan for another treatment unit:
 The plan contains a calculated dose.
 Dose/fraction is defined.
 The plan approval status is one of the following: Planning Approved, 3rd
Party Approved, or Treatment Approved.
 All fields in the plan are photon fields.
 All fields in the plan use the same treatment unit.
Plan sums, cone plans and workflow supported plans cannot be converted for another
treatment unit.

<!-- page 63 -->

The treatment unit used in plan conversion must meet the following requirements:
 The treatment unit has one active MLC configured.
 MLC and the collimator jaws have symmetric operating limits.
 The MLC leaves move in the X direction as in the IEC61217.
 Any non-fixed collimator jaws support asymmetric collimator jaw positions.
 IMRT is supported.”

<!-- page 64 -->

1) Eclipse can quickly convert treatment plans for one treatment unit to another in case the original treatment
unit is unavailable. This method is not meant to replace Plan Revision and the newly created plan should
not be used as the permanent plan.
2) Plans must be Planning, or Treatment Approved before Plan Converter is available.
a) Right click on Plan 1>Plan Approval>Planning Approved. Follow the Plan Approval dialog boxes

3D plans

HIPAA
Varian’s products and services are specifically designed to include features that help our customers comply with the Health
Insurance Portability and Accountability Act of 1996 (HIPAA). The ARIA and VARiS Vision systems use a secure login process,
requiring a user name and password that supports role-based access. Users are assigned to groups, each with certain access
rights, which may include the ability to edit and add data or may limit access to data. When a user adds or modifies data within the
database, a record is maintained of the data that was changed, the users ID and the date and time the changes were made. This
establishes an audit trail that can be examined by authorized system administrators.

<!-- page 3 -->

Eclipse Fair Balance Safety Statement

Intended Use Summary
The Eclipse Treatment Planning System (Eclipse TPS) is used to plan radiotherapy treatments for
patients with malignant or benign diseases. Eclipse TPS is used to plan external beam irradiation with
photon, electron and proton beams, as well as for internal brachytherapy treatments. In addition, the
Eclipse Proton Eye algorithm is specifically indicated for planning proton treatment of neoplasms of the
eye. Eclipse should only be used by qualified medical professionals.

Important Safety Information
Radiation treatments may cause side effects, which in some cases, may be serious.  Severity can vary
depending on the part of the body being treated.  Side effects are related to the type of treatments
delivered and should be discussed between the clinician and the patient.
Medical Advice Disclaimer
Varian as a medical device manufacturer cannot and does not recommend specific treatment
approaches. Individual treatment results may vary.

<!-- page 8 -->

Section 1:  Planning Techniques
Many different planning methods are utilized for treatment delivery:
 Forward planning
 Inverse planning
 Intensity modulated radiation therapy (IMRT) planning
 RapidArc planning

1. Traditional forward planning is usually a trial and error process, where different field and field modifier
setups are tried out to see whether they produce a dose that corresponds to the dose prescription.
a. Setup of field geometry that conforms to the target structure
b. Field modifiers to achieve desired shape of fields and dose distribution
c. Resulting shape of distribution indicated by the dotted line.

 b
 a
 c

<!-- page 9 -->

2. Inverse planning begins by specifying the desired dosimetric outcome of the treatment plan. The inverse
planning engine works backwards from the desired outcome to generate a set of intensity-modulated
fluence matrices. Eclipse optimizes the intensity modulation to produce dose distributions that conform to
the planning target volume as closely as possible
a. Setup of field geometry
b. Definition of optimization objectives to achieve the desired dose
c. Modulated fluence resulting from optimization
d. Resulting shape of dose distribution indicated by the dotted line.

 b
a
 c
 d

<!-- page 10 -->

3. Intensity modulation radiation therapy (IMRT) planning is the act of varying the fluence of a field to shape
the resulting dose distribution. Although the dose distribution of a single IMRT field is typically non-
uniform, the combination of several IMRT fields can result in a very uniform target dose. The Photon
Optimizer algorithm optimizes the beam parameters that lead to the desired dose distribution.

<!-- page 11 -->

4. RapidArc™ planning is Varian’s volumetric modulated arc therapy (VMAT) which requires Eclipse TPS
and Varian treatment delivery systems. RapidArc™ creates a highly conformal volumetric dose
distribution by optimizing gantry rotation speed, dose rate and MLC positions. The resulting arc fields will
have varying gantry speeds, dose rate and field apertures during delivery at the machine.

<!-- page 12 -->

Section 2:  IMRT Planning Overview
IMRT (Intensity Modulated Radiation Therapy) is the delivery method used to create a highly conformal
dose distribution.
During IMRT optimization, the fluences of each field are optimized by changing the beamlet weights.
The field fluences computed by Eclipse are converted into Dynamic MLC (DMLC) motion patterns, and
the modulated dose is delivered with a DMLC device
1. A field is divided into small elements called beamlets.
 A beamlet is the smallest element to be modified.
 The size of the beamlet in X- direction is 0.25 cm at the plane of isocenter.
 The size of the beamlet in Y-direction depends on MLC model represented by the leaf width at the
level of the isocenter.

2. Beamlet values can range from 0 – 1
 0 – intensity:  no dose will be delivered through this beamlet.
 1 – intensity:  dose will be delivered through this beamlet.
 A fluence pattern or intensity map, describes the pattern of changing intensity levels for a field.

<!-- page 13 -->

3. There are different MLC leaf configurations. Leaf width determines the beamlet “height” or width of the
beamlet row.
a. Millennium MLC has 52, 80 and 120 leaf. For the 52 and 80 leaf MLC models: all leaves =1 cm width
at isocenter. For the 120 leaf: 1st and last 10 pairs, leaves are 1 cm width at isocenter. Center 40 pairs
are 0.5 cm width at isocenter.

 a

<!-- page 14 -->

b. HDMLC has 2.5 and 5 mm leaves. Top and bottom 7 cm are 0.5 cm leaves, center 8 cm is 0.25
cm leaves. Maximum length is 22 cm (Y).

 b

<!-- page 15 -->

c. SX1 and SX2 MLC is specific to the Halcyon machine and has 1.0cm width leaves at isocenter. It
has four leaf banks consisting of two stacked layers; proximal and distal that are offset from each
other by 0.5cm. Distal banks are closer to the patient and are the beam defining aperture.
Proximal banks are closer to the source and provide a secondary collimation effect to the distal
leaves. The proximal banks consist of 29 leaves each and the distal banks contain 28 leaves
each for a total of 114 leaves.  The fixed field size for the machine and max leaf travel is
28x28cm.

c

<!-- page 16 -->

Section 3:  IMRT Contouring Overview
IMRT creates highly conformal non-uniform fluence patterns around targets and critical structures. If the
structures are not defined accurately, targets may not be treated as prescribed and critical structures may
receive more than the intended dose.
It is important to review all contoured structures prior to creating an IMRT plan. Ensure structures are
smooth and no structures exist outside of the body. If a stray structure exists outside the main organ
contour, optimization efforts may be hindered. In addition, be mindful of overlapping structures. If
overlapping structures have conflicting objectives, the optimizer may have difficulty achieving the
objectives. In this situation, overlapped regions may need to be cropped and the cropped structure used
for optimization only.
Because of the steep dose gradients at the edge of structures with IMRT and the non-uniform fluence
patterns, patient and target movement can result in a sub-optimal delivery. Correct and precise
delineation of target and critical structures is crucial for IMRT planning. IMRT structures should account
for organ motions.
The two components of motion:
 Internal organ motion (intra-fraction motion)
 Patient setup error (inter-fraction motion)
Margins are important for Targets AND for Organs at Risk
 Setup errors are the same for Organs at Risk
 Organs at Risk move and change shape as well as targets

ICRU 50 and 62 provide guidance on volume definition.  For more information refer to:  Prescribing,
Recording and Reporting Photon Beam Therapy (ICRU 50, 1993);  Supplement, (ICRU 62, 1999) @
www.icru.org
 Gross Tumor Volume — GTV
 Clinical Target Volume — CTV
 Planning Target Volume — PTV
 Internal Margin — IM

To allow for variation in size, shape and position of a structure
 Setup Margin — SM

To allow for uncertainty in patient or beam positioning, set up error
 Internal Target Volume — ITV

Accounts for the variation of size, shape and position of a structure during treatment
 Planning Organ at Risk — PRV

Adds a “safety margin” to structures sensitive to radiation.  It is defined as Organ at risk +
internal margin + setup margin

<!-- page 21 -->

Post Processing

Note: Note: It is very important to Post Process each structure (especially targets).
•
Smooth structures are preferred for IMRT.
•
Small pixels drawn outside of a structure may cause extraneous hotspots.
Post Process the Body – a “hole” in the Body may generate errors during Optimization.

The Post Processing tools are a group of tools used to clean up and smooth structures.  Each of the Post
Processing tools (options) can be used individually or together.
Post Processing tools include:
 Clean-up: Used for removing parts from the active structure. These parts are typically not connected
to the main structure. Parts smaller than a defined size in square or cubic centimeters, parts selected
with the mouse, or overlapping parts can be eliminated.
 Extraction: Keeps defined parts in an active structure. Parts selected with the mouse can be kept.
Parts may be connected or disconnected, keeping the parts exceeding a defined size in centimeters.
When connecting or disconnecting parts, joints may be created between separate segments that
belong to the active structure.
 Enhancement: Removes unwanted details from the active structure. Small spikes protruding from
valleys dipping into the structure selected cavities within a structure can be smoothed. Selected
cavities or cavities smaller than a defined size in square centimeters can be filled.
 The Post Processing tools can be used for the active 2D image, all 2D images or the volume
image.
 Post processing options define how the post processing tool will be applied to the data set. The
2D, 2D All or 3D option must be selected.
 2D: The post processing operations are applied on the active image plane only.
 2D All: The post processing operations are applied on all 2D image planes separately.
 3D: The post processing options are applied to the image volume including the volume
between the 2D image planes.
 Target Structure: The structure that will be Post Processed.

Create Optimization Structures
Whenever structures overlap there is the possibility that one or more of those structures’ doses will
be greater than or less than the desired clinical goal dose for the structure(s).
In the image below, the PTV Prost SV Marg overlaps with the RECTUM.  In the region of overlap,
either the rectal dose will be higher than desired or the PTV Prost SV Marg dose will be lower.
The structure dose that will prevail will depend on that structure’s IMRT or RapidArc Optimization
objectives (priority and dose). Creating optimization structure(s) without the overlap region allows
you to add objectives to non-overlapping structures and/or the areas of overlap. This will allow
Eclipse more freedom to shape the dose distribution.
Several structures without the overlap may be created such as:
 target minus critical structure;
 overlap area-only structure;
 critical structure minus target structure.

IMRT or RapidArc Optimization objectives can be placed on these structures along with the
Optimization objectives to the ‘regular’ target or critical structure. These structures are also called
optimization-only structures.

Section 5:  Field Arrangement Overview
After carefully contouring, post processing and reviewing the structures, you are ready to start creating an
IMRT plan. Plan creation is completed in External Beam Planning.

Note: Navigating to External Beam Planning in this exercise assumes you already have a
patient open. If not, navigate to External Beam Planning using the QuickLinks drop down
menu

1. To create an IMRT plan:
a. Select the External Beam Planning tab.
b. External Beam Planning opens displaying the patient’s 3D image.

b

<!-- page 52 -->

2. In forward planning or conventional planning, you typically use parallel opposed fields.  Parallel opposed
fields allow the system less latitude.
a. In the example on the left, the dose cannot be changed to point C, without modifying points A and B.
During optimization, rays (beamlets) 2 and 2’ compete with each other.  It is not possible to optimize
dose to one point without changing dose to the other points.
b. In the example on the right, the dose to point C can be modified easily without changing points A and
B.  During optimization, the beamlets do not compete with each other.  It is possible to optimize dose
to all points A, B, and C individually.

c. The use of non-coplanar fields may help to significantly decrease the dose to critical structures.
d. Use the Model View in External Beam Planning to setup the fields to avoid entering or exiting through
sensitive structures.

Note: Eclipse will represent the beam arrangement and patient but is not aware of any
possible collisions. Viewing the BEV and Model views while setting up the fields will allow
you to determine the beam placement without collision.

Section 6:  Create an IMRT Plan
You are now ready to create an IMRT Plan. Because your plan will be entering from multiple angles, it is
important to consider the couch and anything else that may be in the path of the beam. You want to be
sure you select the appropriate couch top that is being used for the plan you are creating. Eclipse offers
multiple selections to choose from.
Let’s begin by inserting a couch structure.
Insert Couch Structures
If treatment fields enter through treatment couch structures, the couch will attenuate some of the
treatment beam. This attenuation may cause loss of skin-sparing effect for the patient. With IMRT and
RapidArc, the monitor units may be increased compared to conventional 3D planning. The increased
monitor units may increase the loss of patient skin-sparing.
Insert couch structures when using IMRT or RapidArc techniques.
Before continuing, read the following Notes regarding Couch Structures.

Note: When inserting couch structures in an image:
Make sure that you have selected the correct couch profile, and that the couch structures are
positioned correctly. Visually verify the correctness of couch structures in each image plane.
If the 3D image is too small to fit the couch structures and you choose not to enlarge the
image, some couch structures might not be created completely. This can cause inaccuracies
in dose calculation.
Before using a couch structure for actual treatment, verify the HU values and resulting dose
distribution by using a phantom.
Reference: Eclipse Photon and Electron Instructions for Use P1047678-002-B

Note: Water equivalence data is provided on labels located at specific points on the Exact
IGRT Couch top. These are nominal values only. Qualified personnel should perform
measurements on each machine for dosimetric purposes. All values indicated are
orthogonal to the Exact Couch Top surface.

Section 8: Review Optimization Dialog Box
Intensity-modulated radiotherapy (IMRT) planning in Eclipse creates highly conformal dose distributions
by iteratively optimizing the beam intensity modulation to satisfy a set of user-defined dose volume
objectives. IMRT plans enable you to deliver a relatively uniform dose distribution to a target volume while
sparing surrounding healthy tissue. Eclipse IMRT planning combines intensity modulation and inverse
planning to accomplish this goal.
IMRT optimization requires a dose prescription, at least one field created in External Beam Planning
without MLCs attached.
IMRT optimization takes place in the Optimization Dialog Box. Dose-volume objectives for each target
and critical structure are defined. You can also define fluence smoothing parameters to achieve more
deliverable DMLC sequences and fewer MU to treat the fields.
Following are the different types of optimization objectives used in IMRT:
 Lower objectives: Any structure with a lower dose is considered a target. Lower dose ensures that
the volume receives a minimum dose.
 Upper objectives: Limit the dose to any structure and are used for targets as well as critical
structures. They can be used to decrease the dose a structure receives (such as a percentage dose
to lung or parotid).
 Mean objectives: Are used to decrease the average dose to critical structures.
 gEUD objectives: Generalized Equivalent Uniform Dose (gEUD) is a uniform dose that, if delivered
over the same number of fractions, yields the same radiobiological effect as the non-uniform dose
distribution of interest. Structures with Lower gEUD and Target gEUD objectives are considered to be
targets.

Eclipse identifies the type of optimization needed, either IMRT or RapidArc, by the type of fields that
exists in the selected plan.

Section 9: Define IMRT Optimization Parameters
Objectives:
Objectives are utilized to define the dose goals for a structure.
 Objectives are the dose and volume parameters that are considered optimal for the current patient
and plan.
Objectives can be added manually in the Structures and Objectives section, from an Objective template or
interactively by placing them on the desired location within DVH.
Regardless of the method used to place objectives, they can all be viewed and modified in the tabular
area of Structures and Objectives as well as on the DVH.
Upper objectives:
 Upper objectives are used for both target and critical structures.
 They allow for a maximum dose per unit volume. Example: 50% of RECTUM will receive less than
6000 cGy, 50% of BLADDER will receive less than 6000 cGy, and 0% of the PTV Prost SV Marg
may receive 7673 cGy.
 Numerous upper objectives can be defined per structure. They can be added as needed during
optimization.
 Upper objectives can be modified during optimization.

<!-- page 89 -->

Lower objectives:
 Lower objectives are used for target structures only; it is a minimum dose request. Example: 100% of
PTV Prost SV Marg to receive 7560 cGy.
 Lower objectives are used to determine the treated area; therefore, they cannot be added after
optimization has begun to a structure which did not have a lower objective initially.
 Numerous lower objectives can be defined per structure.

Note: For this plan, we will leave the BLADDER and RECTUM objectives with a priority of
“0” so we can monitor the dose to these structures during optimization.

Important Note: All objectives, doses and priorities are for demonstration purposes only
and are not meant to be recommendations by Varian or its employees.

<!-- page 90 -->

Priorities:
Priority must be assigned to each objective, indicating the structure’s relative importance in the plan.
Objectives within Eclipse range from 0-1000. These values are all relative to each other. This includes
priorities placed on smoothing and minimize dose in optimization.  From 0-100 is demonstrated in this
manual for IMRT.
Priorities can be modified at any time before and during the optimization of IMRT or RapidArc plans.
When adding an objective to any structure, the default priority is ‘0’. Change the priorities as necessary.
One method commonly used to help achieve target coverage is to place higher priorities on the targets
and lower priorities on the critical structures in IMRT Optimization.  After target coverage is achieved,
gradually increase critical structure priorities.

Note: The default Priority is “0”.  In order for Eclipse to optimize a structure, its priority
must be greater than “0”.

<!-- page 91 -->

Volumes:
The structure volumes in cm³ will be displayed in full volume as well as corresponding to the Volume
percentage as objectives are added.
Eclipse randomly adds numbers for the Volume (%) and Dose to structures that are not the target of the
plan. Bladder full volume = 127.1cm³; 50% of Bladder = 61.0cm³ in this example

<!-- page 92 -->

Normal Tissue Objective (NTO)
The Normal Tissue Objective (NTO) is used to limit dose and prevent hot spots in healthy tissue. In
addition, the NTO is used for obtaining a sharp dose gradient around the target volume. An NTO is
calculated for each target structure. The NTO default Priority setting is 100 for IMRT and RapidArc.
With NTO, there is no longer a need to place an upper objective on the Body structure to limit hotspots
outside the target. Also, you no longer need to create concentric dose limiting ring structures around the
target.
 All priorities, for critical structures, targets and the NTO, are relative to each other. Since the range for
priorities is from 0-1000 for targets and critical structures for IMRT, it is important to remember this
relativity.
 Depending on the treatment area, for IMRT, the NTO may be less than, equal to, or occasionally
greater than the priorities of the targets and critical structures. Set the parameters accordingly:

Distance from target border (cm): defines the distance from the target where the system
begins evaluating the dose fall off.

Start dose (%): determines the relative dose level permitted at the start of the NTO, in the
area closest to the target border. This value is relative to the upper objective, not the
prescription dose. If there are multiple upper objectives on a target, the system uses the
lowest upper objective to calculate the start dose.

End dose (%): defines the isodose level permitted in the NTO furthest away from the target
border. This value is also relative to the upper objective on the target.

Fall-off: determines the steepness of the NTO curve shape. Modifying the value in fall off will
essentially change the position of the end dose, closer to or further away from the PTV. It is
helpful to view the position of the end dose in centimeters on the graph.

Modifying these parameters will display the corresponding dose fall off in graphical format.

<!-- page 93 -->

Automatic Normal Tissue Objective in PO
 The system defaults to automatically setting the Normal Tissue Objectives for you. The Automatic
Normal Tissue Objective uses a set of internal parameters. When selected, the parameters in the
Normal Tissue Objectives dialog are not displayed and consequently have no effect on the objective.
The internal parameters depend on the distances of the high dose areas from the target, and they are
adapted dynamically so that they are suitable for the patient anatomy and the objectives during the
optimization. The Automatic Normal Tissue Objective monitors the area within a certain distance
around the target, and if there are doses that are exceptionally high, considering the distance from the
target, it tries to reduce the dose at the area using the user-defined priority value. (From Eclipse
Photon and Electron Algorithms Reference Guide)

<!-- page 94 -->

Manually Define Parameters for the Normal Tissue Objective
To manually set the NTO, deselect the Automatic NTO checkbox and adjust the parameters.

the optimization dialog. This is helpful when structures are present that will not be used or evaluated during
optimization. Exclusion does not remove the structures from the image structure set. Objectives cannot be
added during optimization on excluded structures nor can their DVHs be visualized in the optimization dialog.
Structures can be excluded provided they do not have a lower objective and not used by the NTO (Body).

Note: To improve optimization results, you can calculate an intermediate dose during the
IMRT optimization. This is useful especially for heterogeneous targets, such as lungs. The
intermediate dose is calculated from the current fluences in the plan, using the same dose
calculation algorithm as in the final dose calculation.
You can set IMRT optimization to calculate the intermediate dose automatically during
optimization or you can do it manually at any point during the optimization. In addition, when
re-optimization a plan that already has a dose distribution, you can use the existing dose as
an intermediate dose during the optimization.

The Varian Leaf Motion Calculator (LMCV) Delivery Options. You have 2 Delivery Method choices
– Sliding Window or Multiple Static Segments. Let’s review the choices.

Note: The Leaf Motion Calculator performs several functions:
 Converts Optimal Fluence to Actual Fluence.
 Calculates the position of each leaf for any amount of fractional monitor units.
 Provides a Leaf Sequencing File (STT File) for the delivery device.

<!-- page 125 -->

Delivery Methods
Eclipse External Beam calculation supports 2 delivery methods.
 Sliding Window Delivery
 Sliding Window – This method may also be referred to as Sweeping Gap or Moving Window.  In this
case, two parameters are changed at the same time:
1. Shape of the MLC
2. Delivered Dose
 The leaves are moving while the beam is on. The amount of radiation is controlled by changing the
speed of the leaves and the gap between the leaves.
 During this delivery, the MLC is in dynamic mode (dMLC).
 MLC will first determine the starting position, then the MLC moves across the field, from left to right,
as the dose is delivered.
 This delivery method typically requires more MUs than Multiple Static Segment (MSS) delivery but the
delivery time for sliding window is shorter than MSS.
Multiple Static Segment (MSS) Delivery
 This delivery method is also known as Step and Shoot or Segmental MLC.
 This delivery method is characterized by a series of static MLC shapes (subfields) each with a defined
number of monitor units. The leaves do not move when the beam is being delivered.
 This is another method of the dMLC delivery.
 Only one parameter is changed at a time during this delivery.
1. The shape of the MLC
2. The dose delivered per given segment
 The beam is on hold while the leaves are moving.
 MLC will first create the shape without delivering any dose, then the portion of the MUs will be
delivered to that aperture. The beam is placed in a hold, the shape is modified, then MUs are
delivered to that aperture. This continues until all the segments have been treated.
 Delivering the smallest number of MU does not necessarily mean the shortest delivery time.
 The user can select the desired number of intensity levels in the Leaf Motion Calculation. Intensity
levels do not necessarily equate to the number of segments or subfields. Eclipse will sample the
intensity profile by the defined number and calculate the required number of segments.

Section 13: Normalization
There is No Plan Normalization applied to an inverse planned IMRT plan as a default. The user can select
to normalize as needed to meet the dose objectives. The Leaf Motion Calculator calculates the initial Monitor
Units based on no normalization.
Normalization (or also known as renormalization) can be applied to a plan to increase or decrease the dose
coverage.
The user may wish to achieve specific dose coverage to the target volume. This option may be selected and
the isodose level and volume percentages can be entered.

<!-- page 138 -->

To scale the plan by a specific amount, a value can be entered in the plan normalization value. The value
that appears in this box as a default for IMRT plans is 100% as no default normalization is used. Example:
To scale the plan by 2.0%, a value of 98.0% can be entered in the box.
