Section 14: IMRT Prostate Re-optimization
After completing optimization and evaluating your plan, you may want to re-optimize to attempt to achieve a
better result.  Besides the DVH based optimization, you can also use the Generalized Equivalent Uniform
Dose (gEUD) method.
Let’s take a look.
Generalized Equivalent Uniform Dose (gEUD)
The Generalized Equivalent Uniform Dose (gEUD) is the uniform dose distribution that gives the same
biological effect (clinical effect) equivalent to that of a given heterogeneous dose distribution.
The concept of equivalent uniform dose (EUD) assumes that any two dose distributions are equivalent if they
cause the same radiobiological effect.
In the example below, the red box equals the EUD (dose to a complete volume); finds the dose which results
in the equivalent probability if applied to the whole volume.

<!-- page 142 -->

 This optimization parameter is available with the Photon Optimizer algorithm.
 Parameter ‘a’ is a tissue-specific parameter that describes the volume effect of the gEUD.
 Reference the publications in the labeling for the ‘a’ values: AAPM Report No. 166. March 2012
 The parameter ‘a’ ranges from -40 to 1 for all tumors (targets) and is never 0.
 The parameter ‘a’ ranges from 0 to 40 for all normal structures and is never 0.
 For target and normal structures, ‘a=1’: acts as a mean dose objective. Cold and hot spots are given
equal weight
 The result of using the gEUD for a critical structure is much like adding many upper objectives to it,
each having a different priority.
 The gEUD may be used alone or with DVH-based objectives.

General Equivalent Uniform Dose (gEUD)
The Generalized Equivalent Uniform Dose (gEUD) is the uniform dose distribution that gives the same
biological effect (clinical effect) equivalent to that of a given heterogeneous dose distribution.
The concept of equivalent uniform dose (EUD) assumes that any two dose distributions are equivalent if
they cause the same radiobiological effect.

gEUD uses ‘Parameter a’ to work on the structures’ DVH curves.
 The parameter ‘a’ ranges from -40 to 1 for all tumors (targets) and is never 0.
 The parameter ‘a’ ranges from 0 to 40 for all normal structures and is never 0.
 Using ‘parameter a +10’ is like adding several Upper objectives, with different priorities for them.
 gEUD objectives may be used in combination with the ‘regular’ DVH-based objectives.
 For target and normal structures, ‘a=1” corresponds to the mean dose. Cold and hot spots are given
equal weight.
 ‘Parameter a’ is never ‘0’
 ‘a>1’: Higher doses are given higher weight so that hot spots influence the gEUD to a large extent.
 ‘a=1 or 2: for most parallel responding organs (Lung, Kidney, Parotids, Liver, etc.) behaves like a
mean dose objective.
 ‘a>=8’: for serial responding critical organs, such as rectum, spinal cord, brainstem, or optic nerves.

Section 15: Clinical Protocol
A clinical protocol contains a set of predefined values that guide the treatment planning process. Its purpose
is to speed up treatment planning and to ease the clinician’s workload in typical treatments.
A clinical protocol consists of:
 Structure Template Group – Predefined set of structures.
 Protocol Plans – Predefined set of protocol plans defining the treatment phases (for example the
original treatment and its boost).
 Plan Template – Contains the standard plan characteristics.
 Clinical Goals – Define the intended dose distribution for the target and critical organs realized by
the plan. The overall prescription is a collection of plan objectives that limit the total dose distribution
for the target and critical organs.
 Optimization Objectives – Contains predefined optimization objectives (dose volume objectives).
 Review Settings – Defined structures included in the DVH.

 One clinical protocol can contain both external beam plans and brachytherapy plans.
 Clinical protocols can also be incomplete, as each element of the protocol is not required. It is
possible that a clinical protocol contains only structures, but no clinical goals.
 All users have rights to view clinical protocols. Depending on your user rights, you can also edit,
approve and delete clinical protocols. From Eclipse Photon and Electron Reference Guide
 Clinical protocols are created in the External Beam Planning Application.
 Clinical protocols are created from an existing plan. The information of the active plan is copied to the
protocol, where it may then be accepted or modified.
 Clinical protocols are inserted into patients’ plans via clinical protocol references.
 When a clinical protocol is selected, it is brought into the patient as it was created, and the clinical
protocol reference is assigned to a Course in the Scope window.

<!-- page 155 -->

 Right click on the clinical protocol reference to see the available options.
 Select an option and follow the Wizard. At this point, the clinical protocol options may be changed.
 A clinical protocol can be created which contains one or multiple plans.
 If several plans are to be saved inside a clinical protocol, create a plan sum containing all of the plans
and save the plan sum as a clinical protocol.

<!-- page 162 -->

Prescription Objectives and Clinical Goals
The prescription objectives and clinical goals for a protocol plan define the intended dose distribution for
the target and critical organs, as well as the plan conformity objectives. In clinical goals, you can also
define dosimetric quality parameters for the plan. The overall prescription is a collection of clinical goals
that limit the total dose distribution for the target and critical organs, realized by all the protocol plans in
the clinical protocol.

Note: On the Clinical Goals tab, clinical goals are divided into clinical goals and prescription
objectives. You can add clinical goals and prescription objectives to plans from clinical
protocols.

Clinical protocols have information that you saved from a plan. You can make changes as needed when
you attach a clinical protocol to a patient. You can also edit the clinical protocol as you work with the plan.
One of the prescription objectives is specified as the primary prescription objective.
1.  To edit a prescription item:
a. You can click a cell that has a small arrowhead to modify the information.  Orange cells require
information. Click the cell for the US-10-8FIMRT plan and select PTV Prost SV Margin from the
dropdown.

Note: If the clinical protocol is being created from a plan sum, select each plan separately
to adjust parameters.

<!-- page 163 -->

the remaining cells on the line item.

b

<!-- page 164 -->

Clinical Goals
You can add clinical goals to plans and plan sums from clinical goal templates. Clinical goals include goals for
dose-volume coverage (dose at volume and volume at dose), for maximum, minimum and mean dose, and for
conformity index and gradient measure. The clinical goals define the intended dose distribution for the target
and critical organs. You can also define a variation acceptable value for each clinical goal separately. Clinical
goals are added and modified in the Add/Edit Clinical Goals dialog box. You can add clinical goals from a
clinical goal template, create and modify clinical goals manually, and copy a set of clinical goals to other
plans. You can create clinical goal templates from existing plans that have clinical goals defined.

In addition to adding clinical goals using the Add/Edit Clinical Goals dialog box, you can add clinical goals
from clinical protocols. Clinical goals in plans that are linked to a clinical protocol reference can be modified
only in the Properties of the clinical protocol instance (not in the Plan Properties). All plans created under
the same clinical protocol have the same clinical goals, unless they correspond to different phases in the
protocol. To prevent accidental modifications of clinical goals in a clinical protocol instance, you should
approve the clinical protocol instance.

You can view how the plan fulfills the clinical goals in the following views and applications:

 Clinical Goals tab of the Information Window, which shows a summary of the goals, and from
which you can open a more detailed view of the clinical goals.
 Clinical Goals tab in the Optimization dialog box.
 Multiple Plan Comparison view in Plan Evaluation. In the Multiple Plan Comparison view you can
compare plans and their clinical goals.

Note: Note the following about clinical goals when copying and pasting plans or changing
the clinical protocol linked to the plan:
•
If you change the Protocol Plan to empty in Plan Properties, the clinical goals from
the clinical protocol will be copied to the plan. Prescription objectives will not be
copied.
•
If you assign a plan with defined clinical goals to a clinical protocol phase in the Plan
Properties dialog, the existing clinical goals will be removed.
•
Clinical goals are not copied when you copy a plan to a new image or to an
unregistered image.

<!-- page 165 -->

Examples on How to Define Clinical Goals
Below are some examples of how to define clinical goals in the Add/Edit Clinical Goals, Edit Clinical Goal
Template, and Create Clinical Goal Template dialog boxes.

Tip: Tips for editing the clinical goals:
•
Supported operators are: <, >, ≤, and ≥. The = (equals) operator is not supported.
•
For ≤, type <=. For ≥, type >=. The characters are automatically converted into ≤
and ≥.
•
For Conformity Index, type CI or Conformity Index.
•
For Gradient Measure, type GM or Gradient Measure.
•
For Dmax, Dmin and Dmean, type Dmax/Dmin/Dmean or Maximum dose/Minimum
dose/Mean dose. These clinical goals can be defined using absolute or relative
values.
•
For absolute volume, type cm3 or cc (which is automatically converted to cm3).
•
For dose values, use Gy or cGy, depending on your system configuration.
•
Plan sums do not support clinical goals defined using relative values for dose.

<!-- page 166 -->

Note: For more information regarding Clinical Goals refer to the Eclipse Photon and
Electron Instructions for Use.
Quality Indices
Quality Indices are often used to evaluate Stereotactic plans along with the DVHs and isodose lines.
 Conformity Index is defined as the volume closed by the prescription isodose surface divided
by the target volume.
 Gradient Measure is defined as the difference between the equivalent sphere radius of the
prescription and half-prescription isodoses.
 Dosimetric Quality Parameter describes dose volume coverage for a structure.

Note: For more information regarding quality indices refer to the, Eclipse Photon and
Electron Reference Guide.

c. The third column defines the Objective.
d. The fourth column defines an acceptable Variation to the clinical goal.

Section 16: Irregular Surface Compensation
Electronic compensation involves modulation of the beam with the MLC, rather than using a physical
compensator.
The Irregular Surface Compensator will compensate to a curved surface and compensates for inhomogeneity.
Enable the inhomogeneity correction located in the Calculation Options tab under Plan Properties or in the
Info Window.
The Irregular Surface Compensator is created with the Photon Optimizer (PO) algorithm.
Persistent Dose
Persistent dose is a system wide setting, when enabled, that will allow a user to modify parameters and retain
the isodose distribution.
The setting is system wide, not user or workstation specific.
This feature is useful, for example, if you need to modify blocks, MLCs, fluences or compensators after
reviewing the calculated dose to improve the overall dose distribution.
1. To enable Persistent Dose:

Caution: Modification to the treatment strategy should not be based on outdated or not fully
visualized dose for any plans or plan sums, or dose for plan sums containing weighted
plans.  Refer to the Eclipse Photon and Electron Reference Guide P104679-001-A, page
446.

a. Select Tools.
b. Select Task Configuration…

Fluence Editing Overview
There are two tools available for extending and editing the fluence.
 The Fluence Editor allows the user to sample the fluence values, and then modify brush values to
paint or erase areas of fluence. It also allows for smoothing of the fluence.
 The Skin Flash Tool samples points inside the fluence as the Brush tool is being utilized, so the dose
variation can be reproduced out into the flash.
After calculating the dose, the Optimal Fluence matrix can be displayed as a color wash, gray scale or an
iso-fluence plot overlaid onto a BEV.
Fluence editing involves modification of the Optimal Fluence, which invalidates the dose calculation.
Subsequent recalculation of the dose is required. Persistent Dose located under Tools > Task
Configuration will allow you to retain the isodose visualization after fluence editing. You will be prompted
to recalculate.
Skin Flash Tool
The Skin Flash Tool can be used to prevent sudden fluctuations, such as hot or cold spots, near the body
outline, by extending the Optimal Fluence.
The Skin Flash Tool, which visualizes the fluence of the selected field in the BEV, allows the user to
graphically extend the fluence outside the body surface with a paintbrush. The painted extension is then
filled with transmission values, using the selected fill method and brush parameters.
Portions of the skin flash may be removed with the eraser.
1. To add fluence outside the body:
a. Right click on the Fluence icon of the Med Tang.
b. Select Skin Flash Tool.

b
a

<!-- page 193 -->

The Skin Flash Tool displays:
 The Brush
  is utilized to extend areas of fluence to the skin flash area.
 The Eraser
  is utilized to remove portions of the skin flash area.
 Brush size parameter defines the size of the brush and eraser in millimeters.
 The Measure Transmission tool
  measures and displays the transmission values in the
fluence. The value is displayed in the transmission value text box. The default transmission factor
is 1.
 The Skin Flash tool supports two methods of filling the extension with transmission values,
Nearest cell and Erosion dilation.
 Nearest Cell method: Fills the extension with the transmission value of the nearest cell
beyond the Cut Range limit.
 Erosion-Dilation method: Fills the extension with the transmission values defined by the
Erosion range and Dilation range.
 The Brush ceiling
 parameter defines the upper threshold value
where the fluence extension can be painted. The brush ceiling will define the highest value in the
fluence that cannot be painted over. If the Brush Ceiling value is 0.1, fluence areas with
transmission values of 0.1 and over cannot be painted over.
 The Cut range
 parameter defines the range from which the
transmission values are taken from and measured from the edge of the brush. This parameter
uses the Brush ceiling value when/if the fluence value is greater than the Brush ceiling value. A
low-cut range value finds transmission values from cells close to the edge of the fluence; a higher
cut range value finds values further away from the edge of the fluence.

<!-- page 194 -->

a. Select the Measure Transmission tool, to define the transmission factor of the flash.

Note: The Measure Transmission tool allows to you to sample the fluence near the flash.
This tool is used to locate the minimum value in the existing fluence that will not be replaced
with the paint brush.

Fluence Editor Tool
The Fluence Editor is a tool, which will allow modifications of the transmission values and visualization
including color range, opacity and isodose lines of the fluence.
You can use the Fluence Editor to edit the fluence. The benefit of using this tool is:
 You can edit the fluence (add flash, erase fluence, etc.)
 You can set a value that the paint brush will paint. This does not use the sampling method but paints
the defined value. By using this method, you can avoid the sampling value being extended in areas
you do not want.
 You can define areas to block that you do not want dose delivered. For example, the lung or heart.

2. Brushes:
 When one of the editing tools is selected, a fluence editing brush will become attached to the
cursor, allowing editing of the fluence. Each of these tools will edit the fluence differently.

 When a brush is selected, settings for the tool describing how the fluence will be edited must be
specified.
 The brush diameter may be modified within Tool options.

<!-- page 206 -->

3. Brush Tool options:
 There are 4 types of brush shapes available for editing of the fluence; square, circular, conical,
and Gaussian.

 Circular Brush
  in the Fluence Editor draws a circular area, dragging the brush draws a
line at the end.

<!-- page 207 -->

 Conical Brush
  draws a circular area with linear transmission gradients from the center
towards the edges of the brush tip. Dragging this draws a line with the circular end and linear
gradients from the center of the line toward the edges of the line.

 Gaussian Brush
  draws a circular area with Gaussian transmission gradients from the
center towards the edges of the brush tip. Dragging this draws a line with the circular end and
Gaussian color gradients from the center of the line toward the edges of the line.

<!-- page 208 -->

 Square Brush
  draws a square. Dragging the brush draws a line with a square shaped
end.

<!-- page 209 -->

4. Measure transmission factor (measurement button)
  will allow measurement of fluence values.
 When this tool is selected, the mouse pointer appears as a cross hair in the BEV window.
 When the user selects a point to be measured in the fluence matrix by clicking the left mouse, the
value of the point is displayed in the transmission factor text box.
 When the measure tool is active, the transmission factor box is grayed out and will display the value
of the point selected.
 When one of the brush tools is active, this box becomes active and a value may be entered in the
Transmission Factor box.
 One of the Brush tools can then be utilized to modify the fluence based upon the value set in this box.

<!-- page 210 -->

5. Set transmission factor to given value
 will set the value in the painted area to the selected
transmission factor.
 Any area that is painted will be modified to the transmission value entered by the user.

6. Set transmission factor to 0
  will set the value in the painted area to zero transmission.
 Any area that is painted with this tool will have fluence erased.

<!-- page 211 -->

7. Set transmission factors above given level to defined value
  will set transmission factors
above the existing given level to the defined value.
 If the brush detects any fluence levels above the level defined in the transmission box when
painting fluence, it will lower the painted fluence values to the level defined in the Transmission
box.
 This tool is useful for editing hot spots which may occur on the skin surface for Irregular Surface
Compensation, prior to using the Skin Flash Tool.

<!-- page 212 -->

8. Set transmission factors below given level to defined value
  will set transmission factors
below the given value to the defined value.
 If the brush detects any fluence levels below the level defined in the transmission box, it will
increase those values to the level defined in the transmission box.

<!-- page 213 -->

9. Increase transmission factors by defined value
 will increase the transmission factors under the
brush by the value defined in Transmission Factor.
 This tool will allow the user to paint “layers” of fluence on top of existing fluence.
 To add additional layers of fluence, the apply button must be selected after painting. If the user
paints over a previously painted area without selecting the apply button, only one layer of fluence
will be added.

<!-- page 214 -->

10. Decrease transmission factors by defined value
 will decrease the transmission factors under
the brush by defined value.
 This tool will allow the user to subtract “layers” of fluence from the existing fluence. To subtract
additional layers of fluence, the Apply button must be selected after painting. If the user paints over a
previously painted area without selecting the apply button, only one layer of fluence will be
subtracted.
 This tool is helpful in editing fluence to decrease hot spots on a plan.

<!-- page 215 -->

11. Build up transmission factors by defined value
 will add additional layers with the same
transmission factor on top of each other, by painting over the previously painted areas.
 This is similar to the ‘increase by defined value tool’, as it adds layers to the fluence. The apply button
for this tool is not required to add additional layers. Use low transmission values as each movement
of the mouse adds a layer from the fluence.

<!-- page 216 -->

12. Erase transmission factors by a defined value
  will subtract additional layers with the same
transmission factor from the fluence, by painting over the previously painted areas.
 Use low transmission values as each movement of the mouse subtracts a layer from the fluence.

<!-- page 217 -->

13. Smooth transmission factors
 smooths out sharp changes in transmission values and can be
used to remove small irregularities.

<!-- page 218 -->

14. Visualization
 Use colors: displays the fluence in color, which enables the user to distinguish hotter areas from
colder areas within the fluence.
 Isolevels will connect points with the same intensity.
 ‘Use Shading’ tool displays the fluence in 3D instead of 2D.
 Upper and Lower values will range the fluence display. Any values outside the selected range will not
be displayed.
 Opacity will change the transparency of the fluence display.

Editing a hot spot
Determine which Fluence Editor Tool (brush) will be utilized for editing the fluence. The most commonly
used tools for editing fluence to decrease a hot spot are:
 Decrease transmission factors by defined value
, which removes small layers of fluence in
the painted area. This tool is helpful for reducing hot spots within the dose distribution.
 Set transmission factors above given value to defined value tool
, which decreases the
painted area to the threshold value set in Transmission Factor. This tool is helpful for decreasing any
areas of increased fluence on the skin surface from the Irregular Surface compensator. This tool can
also be utilized to decrease hot spots within the dose distribution.

<!-- page 232 -->

Normalizing the plan
If the Prescribed Percentage value and/or Plan Normalization value is modified by more than 5% after the
initial leaf motion calculation, the leaf motion calculation and final dose distribution must be recalculated. This
is necessary to update the leaf sequencing pattern based upon the new monitor units. Always rerun the leaf
motion calculator if treating on a non-Varian machine.
For Electronic and Irregular Surface Compensators, (forward planned IMRT) the Leaf Motion Calculator uses
the system-wide normalization default, when calculating the initial monitor units and leaf motions. It will ignore
any automatic or manual renormalization selected by the user when calculating the initial monitor units.
Resulting leaf motions will be based upon the system default normalization.
Therefore, the user cannot avoid recalculating leaf motions (if renormalizing greater than 5%), simply by
selecting a renormalization option prior to dose calculation. If calculated and renormalized MUs are present
and the LMC is rerun, the system will base the leaf motion calculation on the existing MU.
1. Determine if the plan is being scaled greater than 5%.

Note: When normalizing to a reference point or an isocenter, the user may be uncertain as
to the percent the plan has been renormalized.
For forward planned IMRT, the Leaf Motion Calculator uses the system-wide normalization
default to determine the initial monitor units and leaf motions. Removing any subsequent
normalization will display initial monitor units and normalization that the system used to
calculate leaf motions. This makes it easier to determine the amount the plan has been
rescaled.
a. Select Planning
b. Select Plan Normalization.

Prescribing to an isodose level
If the physician elects to prescribe to an isodose level other than 100, it may be entered in the Dose
Prescription tab under Prescribed Percentage.

Note: Modifying this value will scale the monitor units and Absolute Dose. It will not scale
the relative isodose lines. If the prescribed dose changes by more than 5% it is
recommended to rerun the leaf motion calculator and recalculate the plan. Remember to
always select No Normalization before changing the normalization options.

Focus on Structure
Focus on Structure is a feature in the optimization window that allows the user to align the 2D view to the
structure dose or structure center of interest.
There are 4 options:
 No Focus on Structure
 Focus on Structure Center
 Focus on Structure Maximum Dose
 Focus on Structure Minimum Dose

Avoidance Structures
1. Add Avoidance Structures for the left and right parotids.
 The avoidance structure tool in PO optimization allows you to block the radiation beam when
avoidance structures are in front of or behind a target. It can be used for both IMRT and VMAT
techniques and can be combined with avoidance sectors and upper objectives.
 Avoidance structure tool is a way to block radiation pixel by pixel in a fluence pane. The avoidance
structure is projected onto the fluence plane, and if the radiation through a certain fluence pixel hits
the avoidance structure before or after hitting the target, the radiation through this fluence pixel is
blocked.
 In an IMRT plan, the fluence pixel is blocked by setting the pixel value of the fluence to zero. In a
VMAT plan, the fluence pixel is blocked by moving the leaves on top of the pixel, which also blocks all
the radiation along the leaf. Leaf speed rules can cause some small leave opening to show up on top
of avoidance structures when leaves need to be moved from one side of the projection to another.
 For VMAT ONLY: Another way to block radiation is to use an avoidance sector, but it blocks the
radiation from the whole fluence plane. A situation where both avoidance sector and avoidance
structure are useful together would be a VMAT lung case, where the avoidance sector can be set to
avoid a significant volume of healthy lung, yet avoidance structure can be used to avoid entrance
radiation through hardware inserted to the spine, or both entry and exit to the spinal cord.

a. Select PAROTID_LT.
b. Click Avoidance Structure
 icon.
c. Click

<!-- page 259 -->

Target Autocrop
This tool automatically optimizes whole simultaneous Boost Targets without the user having to create
optimization targets and corresponding objectives manually.
It works with both IMRT and VMAT techniques but does not work with the following tools:
•
Multi-Criteria Optimization
•
SRS NTO
•
Automatic Lower Dose Objectives (ALDO)
The feature searches for target structure overlaps and if any overlapping targets have contradicting objective
settings (one asking for less and one asking for more dose inside the same volume), the algorithm
automatically creates artificial Boolean parts out of the target volume that is asking less dose. The target
asking for more dose will always dictate the dose in the overlapping area.
A 5mm internal margin structure is created around the higher dose target to allow the dose to ramp-up from
the lower target dose to higher one. The algorithm also automatically converts the dose objectives for the
internal structures. This means that the upper dose objective’s volume level, normally manually calculated for
the lower dose level target, can now be set directly to 0-volume level, as if there were no overlap.
The optimization DVHs shown are calculated normally for the whole structure volumes. The graphic below
illustrates how Target Autocrop works. The X-axis in the dose-volume chart is a cross section across the
PTV-volumes after the automatic Boolean has done its job.

To use this tool, follow these rules:
1. Use this tool on a case where there are two or more overlapping targets with conflicting objectives.
2. Ensure you only have one upper (0% volume) and one lower (100% volume) objective for each
overlapping target. There are no restrictions to the number of objectives for additional non-overlapping
targets.

<!-- page 260 -->

3. Select the Target Autocrop icon from the optimization window tool bar prior to starting the optimization.

4. Internally, the target is cropped with a 5mm margin and additional dose objectives to drive the
optimization for the cropped target are created automatically. This cropped structure is not displayed nor
displayed on the DVH. The DVHs displayed in the DVH window display the whole targets. The objective
pane also displays the whole target.

<!-- page 261 -->

a. The Target Autocrop icon is displayed next to the targets that were used by the algorithm once the
optimization begins.

b. It is not possible to modify the dose levels of lower objectives of overlapping targets once optimization
is started. However, you can delete upper objectives of overlapping targets during optimization.

c. To remove the cropped targets, deselect the Target Autocrop icon and restart the optimization. The
Target Autocrop icons will disappear once the optimization begins.

Section 22: Partial IMRT Treatment Plan
You can reconstruct partially treated IMRT or RapidArc plans in Eclipse for assessing the dosimetric and
clinical implication of partial treatments. In treatment reconstruction, you create a partial plan from a plan that
contains dynamic MLCs. The partial plan contains the delivered portion of the original plan.
The partial plan is a verification plan. It is a copy of the original plan with the following exceptions:
 The actual fluences and existing dose distribution are removed
 The plan has only the delivered portion of control points
 The plan has only one fraction
 The dose will be calculated using the delivered number of MU

When creating a partial plan, you can choose to also create a second verification plan that has one fraction
and full MU. This plan can be compared with the partial plan.
1. For this example, continue with patient, EC-2012, EIPOPS, HEADNECK, in the External Beam Planning
application. Use the US-13_HN 9# plan.
2. For this procedure, it is assumed that the plan has been partially treated:
 Fields 1-5 were treated with planned MUs.
 Field 6 was treated with 49 MUs only.
 Fields 7--9 were not treated.

Section 23: Halcyon Planning
Halcyon Overview
A Halcyon treatment unit has a fixed primary collimator and a rotating secondary collimator. Halcyon beam
collimation modeling in Eclipse is based on the following main parts:
 Fixed 28 X 28 cm square that represents a non-rotating primary collimator.
 Rotating secondary collimator with fixed jaws.
 Rotating multi-leaf collimator SX1/SX2 MLC with dynamic leaves (rotates with the secondary
collimator).
The Halcyon collimation system has a square shaped fixed primary collimator which results in the maximum
field size of 28 X 28 cm in the isocenter plane. The full field size can only be achieved at -90⁰, 0⁰ and 90⁰
secondary collimator rotation. The leaf position limits for SX1 and SX2 MLC allow leaf openings only within
the primary collimator aperture. The constraints ensure that the rotating collimator (and MLC) covers the non-
rotating primary collimator with the MLC leaves at all times.
The 1 cm leaves in the SX1 and SX2 MLC are organized in MLC banks. Both the SX1 and SX2 MLC have
four leaf banks in two layers, proximal and distal that are offset by 0.5 cm. By default, the MLC leaves are
visualized as wireframes in the BEV. When solid MLC leaves are shown, the proximal MLC leaves are
displayed with lighter color, and the distal leaves with darker color.

In the SX1 MLC, the proximal leaves cannot shape the beam. Therefore, when proximal leaves are limited to
the boundary of the primary collimator, the overlapping distal leaves are limited to the same position as the
proximal leaves. Each distal leaf is limited by two overlapping proximal leaf positions. In the SX2 MLC, both
distal and proximal leaves can shape the beam. Both distal and proximal leaves are limited to the boundary of
the primary collimator. Fluence resolution in optimization for the SX1 MLC is 1 cm, and for the SX2 MLC it is
0.5 cm. The fit and shield tool cannot be used with the SX1 or SX2 MLC.

<!-- page 326 -->

Note: In Halcyon planning, avoid creating apertures where interleaf leakage is not limited
by the other MLC layer.

Treatment plans for Halcyon treatment units using SX1 or SX2 MLC can contain the following treatment
techniques.
 Dynamically flattened beam (Fixed Sequence) MLC technique, where the distal layer of the MLC
leaves is used for defining a static MLC aperture and the proximal layer is used for flattening the
FFF beam profile.
 VMAT
 IMRT sliding windows.
 Field-in-field
 Irregular surface compensator
Other treatment field types and MLC techniques are not supported.

Note: For Halcyon planning with the Irregular Surface Compensator: Make sure that the
static MLC aperture is set appropriately as it defines the boundary for the resulting
compensator. The final compensator outline follows the static MLC aperture with the
accuracy of 2.5 mm (size of a fluence pixel). If you are using the SX2 MLC, removing the
dynamic beam flattening enables you to edit the MLC aperture with a half-leaf width (5 mm)
accuracy in the Y direction (perpendicular to leaf axis).

<!-- page 327 -->

Dynamic Beam Flattening
Halcyon treatment units support a flattening filter free (FFF) beam. The technique where a layer of MLC
leaves is employed for flattening the beam is called dynamic beam flattening. In the dynamic beam flattening
techniques, the distal layer of the MLC leaves is used for defining a static MLC aperture and the proximal
layer is used for flattening the FFF beam profile. The static leaf position in the distal MLC layer can be edited,
but the MLC sequence in the proximal MLC layer used for dynamic beam flattening is fixed. The flattening
sequence compensates the FFF beam profile with sliding window type of leaf motion.
The sequences are designed to maintain relatively efficient modulation factors, while still providing clinically
acceptable delivered flatness. The minimum MU required to deliver a dynamic flattening sequence on
Halcyon is 10 MU. This should be taken into consideration when using a Field-in-Field technique when the
flattening sequence is required and the sub-fields will not be merged.

The BEV always shows the complete irradiated area outline (CIAO) for the distal layer with the static aperture.
The CIAO is defined by the leaves in the distal MLC layer while the leaves in the proximal MLC layer define
the flattening sequence. By default, a new Halcyon field is a static aperture with dynamic beam flattening
(Fixed Sequence) MLC technique. The dynamic flattening sequence is removed automatically:
 If the field technique is changed to ARC
 When an irregular surface compensator is added to the field
 In the IMRT optimization where the optimal fluence replaces the dynamic flattening sequence.

Note: You can remove dynamic flattening from the treatment field by right-clicking the MLC
of the field and choosing Remove Flattening Sequence. Fields with dynamic flattening
cannot be merged in the field-in-field technique. Dynamic flattening (fixed sequence MLC) is
automatically removed during the IMRT, VMAT, and irregular surface compensator
workflows.

Tip: To add the beam flattening back, right-click the static MLC and choose Add Flattening
Sequence.

<!-- page 328 -->

Imaging and Boluses in Halcyon Plans
A Halcyon plan automatically creates imaging fields that are used for patient positioning and treatment fields
when the plan is created. You can manually link one bolus or several boluses to a Halcyon plan (linking is
done on a plan level, not field level). When a bolus is linked to a plan, all imaging fields and treatment fields in
the plan are linked to the same bolus. If you unlink a bolus from a Halcyon plan, the link is removed from all
imaging fields and treatment fields.
In Halcyon plans, the MV imaging fields have prescribed numbers of MU. The kV imaging fields do not have
prescribed MU. In order to achieve optimal therapeutic dose distribution, the treatment dose needs to be
optimized taking into consideration the planned MV imaging dose. The goal of the plan optimization for a
Halcyon treatment unit is to generate a treatment plan where base dose, MV imaging dose and optimized
treatment dose, when combined, meet the objectives defined for plan optimization. Imaging setups can be
changed after the plan is created.

Note: It is recommended to re-optimize a Halcyon plan after changing the imaging setup.

Halcyon plans are scheduled automatically in plan approval. In automatic scheduling, session scheduling of
plans and imaging sequences is done based on Halcyon-specific imaging procedure templates. The
automatic scheduling is performed only when the course contains no other scheduled plans or any treated
plans. If the automatic scheduling succeeds, you do not need to go to Plan Scheduling.
Halcyon Couch Modeling in Eclipse
Halcyon couch structures are created automatically in the plan image when a new plan is created. The
Halcyon couch cannot be rotated and is always 0 degrees. The couch profile extends the whole image in the
Z direction. The shape and dimension in the X-Y section is uniform through the image in the Z direction.
Couch structures are taken into account in dose calculation and optimization.
Halcyon Collision Detection in Eclipse
Eclipse contains Halcyon-specific collision check that can prevent plan optimization, dose calculation and plan
approval. If there is a risk of collision, the bore of the Halcyon treatment unit is highlighted in red in the 2D
views. The outer circle in the image views represents the physical bore, and the collision is checked against
the body structure. The inner circle represents the safety margin, which is checked against the couch.

<!-- page 329 -->

Planning for Long Targets with Halcyon
It is possible to treat targets which are larger in the longitudinal direction than Halcyon treatment unit’s 28 X
28 cm collimator (at isocenter) by utilizing multiple isocenters in a single plan. Halcyon multi-isocenter plans
can be planning and treatment approved if they adhere to the following rules:
 Maximum of two treatment field isocenters and one imaging field isocenter.
 Depending on the Halcyon treatment unit, the maximum distance between treatment isocenters is
either 8 cm (Halcyon 2.0) or 10.5 cm (Halcyon 3.0) providing a total length of the combined fields
at isocenter of 36 cm or 38.5 cm.
 Imaging field isocenter is at or between the two treatment isocenters.
 All isocenters are in the same longitudinal axis (that is, not shifted laterally or anterior/posterior
with respect to each other).
You can use the Arrange as Multi-Isocenter Setup tool to easily create multiple isocenters in a Halcyon
plan. (RMC on the Isocenter group in the focus window). The tool creates a multi-isocenter setup with three
isocenter groups and the isocenters are aligned to the same superior/inferior axis. The imaging isocenter is
placed at the location of the original isocenter group in the plan, and the new treatment isocenters are placed
either at 4 cm or 5.25 cm distance on both sides of the imaging isocenter. Existing fields are copied to the
second treatment isocenter.
Automatic feathering can be applied in plan optimization to create smooth dose gradients in plan with multiple
isocenters where the treatment fields overlap. Automatic feathering functionality strives to reduce the error
caused by patient position inaccuracies. More information on automatic feathering can be found in Eclipse
Photon and Electron Algorithms Reference Guide.

2 Phase IMRT Prostate Plan
Using the following information create a 2-phase prostate plan:
 Original plan (prostate and SV + margin)
 5400 in 30 TX  71.5% of the dose
 Boost (prostate + margin)
 2160 in 12 TX  28.5% of the dose

 7560 in  42 TX

maximum Dose not to exceed
110%

maximum Dose not to exceed
110%

PTV Prostate + SV margin

(95%) 5130  (110%) 5940

(CTV) Prostate

(95%) 2052  (110%) 2376

External Beam Planning
1) From User Home > Quicklinks > External Beam Planning.
2) Select EC-016 Prostate, William in the Patient Explorer Dialog Box.
3) Open 3-D image EIP Ops contours in the Object Explorer Dialog Box.

<!-- page 348 -->

4) Review previously created contours.
5) Review the location of target structures and avoidance structures.
6) Insert new couch structures.
Create a plan from a template using a beam arrangement of your choice.
1) Enter and verify the dose prescription.
2) Verify inhomogeneity correction for the PO.
3) Verify the beam energy and dose rate.
Optimization
1) This will be an Interactive IMRT. Create plans that you would show to a physician.
2) Enter objectives to achieve clinical goals, using a Prostate objective template Set the NTO options.
3) Start Optimization.
4) During Optimization, modify the objectives to achieve better dose distribution.
5) When the Overall Objective Function becomes static, run the Intermediate Dose calculation.
6) Select the Automate optimization options and allow the IMRT to complete.
To External Beam Planning
1) Review and evaluate final 3D dose
2) Renormalize Dose Distribution
3) Evaluate DVH to verify if clinical goals given in the exercise are met, normalizing or modifying plan as
needed.
Renormalize as needed.
1) Rerun LMC and final dose calculation as needed.
2) Evaluate plan using a Dose Color Wash.
3) Perform a surface dose on the PTV.
Phase 2 Plan
1) Perform the proceeding steps to create and optimize an IMRT plan for the boost.
2) Evaluate and normalize the boost plan.
3) Sum the original and the boost plan together and evaluate the summed plan.
4) Show your plans to your instructor.

<!-- page 349 -->

IMRT Head and Neck with Separate Bilateral Supraclavicular Field & Optimizing with a Base-
Dose plan
Objective
 Using the Eclipse IMRT course manual, the students will create and review a head and neck
IMRT plan using a beam-split supraclavicular plan as a Base dose plan.
Procedure Contents
 Open an existing patient EIPOPS, HEADNECK (US-EC-2012)
 Review previously created head and neck contours
 Review clinical goals
 Review a previously created supraclavicular field in Course 1, recalculate with Default calculation
models and ensure you select the same machine for both plans.
 Create an IMRT head and neck plan to treat the PTVs.
 Optimize the plan using the AP SCLAV PLAN as a Base Dose Plan
 Create a Plan Sum
 Review the Plan Sum
In some cases, you need to take into account a certain base dose for the optimization, for instance, in a boost
treatment or in this case, if the physician wants a separate AP field to treat the supraclavicular nodes. You
can define the total dose and let the optimization create a plan that selectively doses the optimized plan
considering the calculated dose of the base dose plan. The optimization extracts the base dose from the total
dose objectives when the dose objectives are evaluated and returns the optimal fluence for the optimized
plan.
The base dose plan can be any treatment plan, or a plan sum, with the following conditions:
 The plan or plan sum must have the same planning image as the current plan
 The dose distribution must be calculated.
 The dose prescription must be defined.
If a plan sum is used as a base dose plan, the plan sum must meet the following additional conditions:
 The plan sum may not contain the plan that is currently under optimization.
 All the plan weights in the plan sum must be 1.0 and no minus operations are used in the sum.

Tip: If you need to remove a base dose plan from the second plan, click Clear Base Dose
in the Base Dose Plan drawer.

<!-- page 350 -->

External Beam Planning
1) From User Home > Quicklinks > External Beam Planning.
2) Select EIPOPS, HEADNECK (US-EC-2012) in the Patient Explorer Dialog Box.
3) Open AP SCLAV PLAN in the Object Explorer Dialog Box. Recalculate with default calculation
models.
4) Review previously created contours.
5) Review the location of target structures and avoidance structures.
Create a Head and Neck plan from a template using a beam arrangement of your choice.
4) Enter and verify the dose prescription.
5) Verify inhomogeneity correction for the PO.
6) Verify the beam energy and dose rate.
Optimization
1) This will be an Interactive IMRT. Create plans that you would show to a physician.
2) Enter objectives to achieve clinical goals using a Head and Neck objective template. Set the NTO
options.
3) In the Optimization window, open the Base Dose Plan objective expander, select the AP SCLAV
PLAN from the list.
4) Start Optimization.
5) During Optimization, modify the objectives to achieve better dose distribution.
6) When the Overall Objective Function becomes static, run the Intermediate Dose calculation.
7) Select the Automated optimization options and allow the IMRT to complete.
To External Beam Planning
1) Review and evaluate final 3D dose for the Head and Neck plan.
2) Renormalize Dose Distribution
3) Insert a Plan Sum to include the AP SCLAV PLAN and the Head and Neck Plan.
4) Evaluate DVH to verify if clinical goals given in the exercise are met, normalizing or modifying plan as
needed.
Renormalize as needed.
1) Rerun LMC and final dose calculation as needed.
2) Evaluate plan using a Dose Color Wash.

<!-- page 351 -->

3) Compare your Plan Sum to your other plan you optimized without the AP SCLAV PLAN in Plan
Evaluation.
4) Show your final Plan Sum to your instructor.

Eclipse Treatment Planning System Fair Balance Safety Statement

Intended Use Summary
The Eclipse Treatment Planning System (Eclipse TPS) is used to plan radiotherapy treatments for patients
with malignant or benign diseases. Eclipse TPS is used to plan external beam irradiation with photon, electron
and proton beams, as well as for brachytherapy treatments. In addition, the Eclipse Proton Eye algorithm is
specifically indicated for planning proton treatment of neoplasms of the eye. Eclipse should only be used by
qualified medical professionals.
Important Safety Information
Radiation treatments may cause side effects, which, in some cases, may be serious. Severity can vary
depending on the part of the body being treated. Side effects are related to the type of treatments delivered
and should be discussed between the clinician and the patient.
Medical Advice Disclaimer
Varian as a medical device manufacturer cannot and does not recommend specific treatment approaches.
Individual treatment results may vary.

Smart Segmentation
Smart Segmentation is the Eclipse workspace used for auto-contouring structures, based on using Expert
Cases in the system.  Eclipse comes with many expert cases in the system that are ready for use by
treatment planners and physicians.  Eclipse allows the creation of Expert Cases by the user as well.

Section 1: Smart Segmentation Overview
1. Navigate to Smart Segmentation, using QuickLinks.

1

<!-- page 6 -->

2. For this example, open patient US-EC-3001.

3. Double click on the CT icon labeled FOR SMART SEG.

2
3

<!-- page 7 -->

Tools found in the Contouring workspace are also available here.  Manual contouring as well as
edits to the auto-contouring can be done from here.

Select Expert Case for Segmentation

5. To begin auto contouring click on the Select Expert Case for Segmentation icon in the tool bar.

in the sections.
9. The Expert Cases that meet the selected filters appear at the bottom of the window. Since we have
not selected any filters, there are currently 155 Expert Cases found in the system.

10. From the Tumor Site menu, select HypoPharynx.

12. Notice the Similarity score (it is based on a scale of 1 to 5) the number of blue dots indicates the
Similarity score.

Note:  The Similarity score is based upon the similarity of the Patient’s CT data set with
that of the Expert Case CT. A value of 4 or 5 dots indicates a high similarity of the of the
patient’s CT with that of the Expert Case.
Eclipse evaluates the CT data in both the patient data set and the Expert Case CT.  It is
smart enough to determine if the anatomy in the CT data sets is similar.
For example, if you were to select Prostate as the Tumor Site for this patient, the similarity
would be zero (no blue dots).  The system is smart enough to recognize the CT data is not
similar at all.

13. The system will automatically select multiple Expert Cases.  For this example, the system has
selected 3 cases as indicated by the checkboxes.

20. Based on the higher similarity of 3 blue dots, the top case might be the better Expert Case to use for
contouring this patient.

Note:  Smart Segmentation allows you to use multiple cases as a basis for auto
contouring.  The user needs to select or deselect the check boxes for the Expert Cases
they wish to use.  In this case, if the user only wanted to use one case, they would need to
deselect one of the check boxes.
The greater the number of cases selected, the longer the algorithm takes to come up with
the best contour for that structure based on the expert cases selected.
The gain in accuracy will diminish after a certain number of cases. This will vary with patient
and is highly dependent upon the similarity of the patient’s image and the Expert Cases
images.  The Similarity dots are helpful in identifying similarity.

Structure generation options

 Propagate from Expert Case

Note:  Smart Detection contours the structure using smart pixel recognition and may result
in more accurate contours for that structure.
Propagate from Expert Case copies the structure from the Expert case onto the patient’s
CT after a deformable registration has occurred in the background.
The user should decide which method provides the most accurate contour for the structure.
Reminder: Auto contours should always be verified and edited manually as needed.

Section 1:  RapidArc™ Treatment Delivery
RapidArc™ Delivery
RapidArc™ is a unique delivery technique in which the Gantry moves continuously during treatment
delivery while other parameters are changed.

RapidArc delivers the dose using variable dose per degree and dynamic MLCs.
 The variable dose per degree is achieved by modulating either the dose rate (preferably) or
Gantry rotation speed.
 The linear accelerator control system will move the Gantry at maximum speed as much as
possible to minimize the treatment time.
 Delivered dose distribution does not depend on the dose rate or speed of motions as long as the
dose vs. position relationship is maintained between two control points.

The treatment is controlled by Clinac and MLC controller on C-series linacs.
 The Clinac controller is responsible to maintain the MU vs. Gantry position relationship extracted
from DICOM control points.
 The MLC controller maintains MLC vs. Gantry relationship extracted from the same DICOM
control points.
 The information transferred to the Clinac and MLC controller is created by 4DITC based on
DICOM RT plan information.

<!-- page 10 -->

The treatment on TrueBeam machine is controlled by the Supervisor which manages all nodes and
motion axes included in treatment.
 The Supervisor receives the control points from DICOM plans and sends orders for appropriate
actions from the machine nodes. The orders are proactively sent to the nodes for two heartbeats
in advance. Each node executes the orders with multitude frequency and reports the axis status
back to the Supervisor.
To deliver RapidArc, the delivery system requires information about the dose versus the Gantry position
and at the same time, the Gantry position versus MLC leaves positions.
 Control points are used to define this relationship.
 Each full arc is comprised of 178 control points. (Smaller arcs may have less control points).
 At each control point the system knows the maximum dose rate (set in Field Properties), the
maximum Gantry speed, the number of MU for the portion of the segment and the starting and
ending MLC apertures.
 The system calculates a trajectory for Gantry rotation and MU based on linear interpolation
between the control points.
Constraints for Delivery
The optimization and delivery are constrained by machine limits.  Several parameters must not be
exceeded:
1. Maximum speed of rotation:
 The maximum rotation speed in clinical mode is 0.8 RPM (4.8 deg/s) for C-series linacs.
TrueBeam rotates at maximum speed of 1 RPM during RapidArc delivery (6 deg/s). Eclipse uses
a value defined in operating limits – if no value is defined 4.8deg/s is used.
Maximum Leaf speed:
 Typically, 2.5 cm/s speed is used, and the Algorithm reduces the speed by 10% behind the
scenes.  The 10% calculation is taken from the MLC properties in RT Administration.
Maximum Dose rate per field – defined in Field Properties:
Maximum MU per degree:
 20MU/deg or 60MU/deg if SRS beam is used;
Minimum MU per degree:
 0.1MU/deg.

<!-- page 11 -->

The optimization also includes constraints for efficiency.
 The Gantry should move at maximum speed to decrease treatment time.
 The dose should be modulated by dose rate variation rather than Gantry speed variation because
of inertia of the Gantry.
 The optimization will not exceed the dose rate specified in field parameters. If more dose needs to
be delivered, the Gantry rotation will be slowed down (within limits). It is recommended to use the
maximum dose rate available for the given energy mode. This will increase the dynamic range of
modulation. The Gantry rotation acceleration/ deceleration is constrained because of large inertia
of the Gantry.

<!-- page 12 -->

Progressive Resolution
The optimization is performed in 4 resolution levels.
 The multi-resolution scheme applies to calculation directions only
 The number of calculation directions doubles at each resolution level
 Number of control points does not change.  It is set at the beginning of the optimization
 The control points are created by the MLC.  The initial MLC shapes are conformed to the targets
 Algorithm flexibility preserved through optimization
 The initial dose rates are equal for all dose calculation segments.
 Larger MLC changes are available during the earlier levels. The size of the MLC adjustments
diminish as the optimization progresses through the levels.
 The DVH is equally accurate for OARs and targets from the very beginning of optimization.
 Only the calculation directions follow the multi-resolution scheme.
 The dosimetric accuracy increases with the resolution level.
Control points which “belong” to a calculation direction are used to calculate fluence. This is used for
a dose calculation from the calculation direction.  Control points within single calculation direction
merged into “fluence” for given calculation direction

Initial Resolution

Final Resolution

Control Points

<!-- page 13 -->

Progressive Steps
The Photon Optimizer optimizes the fluence for each calculation direction. The algorithm corrects for the
change in parameters by modifying beam parameters
 The calculation directions are independent at the beginning of optimization.
 The machine limits must be enforced on the calculation direction interface. All machine limits are
enforced within each calculation direction representing the arc segment.
 During each optimization iteration, leaf sequencing is done for each calculation direction.

A gradient method is used.
 This is done gradually with the increasing steps of optimization.
 Within each step, machine limits at the calculation direction interface are gradually enforced by
increasing penalty. As a result, the penalty function abruptly increases when the segment is
changed.

<!-- page 14 -->

Section 2:  RapidArc™ Contouring Overview
RapidArc™ creates highly conformal non-uniform fluence patterns around targets and critical structures. If
the structures are not defined accurately, targets may not be treated as prescribed and critical structures
may receive more than the intended dose.
It is important to review all contoured structures prior to creating a RapidArc™ plan. Ensure structures are
smooth and no structures exist outside of the body.  If a stray structure exists outside the main organ
contour, optimization efforts may be hindered.  In addition, be mindful of overlapping structures. If
overlapping structures have conflicting objectives, the optimizer may have difficulty achieving the
objectives.  In this situation, overlapped regions may need to be cropped for optimization purposes only.
Because of the steep dose gradients at the edge of structures with RapidArc™ and the non-uniform
fluence patterns, patient and target movement can result in a sub-optimal delivery.  Correct and precise
delineation of target and critical structures is crucial for RapidArc™ planning.  RapidArc™ structures
should account for organ motions.
The two components of motion:
 Internal organ motion (intra-fraction motion)
 Patient setup error (inter-fraction motion)

Margins are important for Targets AND for Organs at Risk
 Setup errors are the same for Organs at Risk
 Organs at Risk move and change shape as well as targets

ICRU 50 and 62 provide guidance on volume definition.  For more information refer to:  Prescribing,
Recording and Reporting Photon Beam Therapy (ICRU 50, 1993);  Supplement, (ICRU 62, 1999) @
www.icru.org
 Gross Tumor Volume—GTV
 Clinical Target Volume—CTV
 Planning Target Volume—PTV
 Internal Margin—IM
 To allow for variation in size, shape and position of a structure
 Setup Margin—SM
 To allow for uncertainty in patient or beam positioning, or set up error
 Internal Target Volume—ITV

Accounts for the variation of size, shape and position of a structure during treatment
 Planning Organ at Risk—PRV

Adds a “safety margin” to structures sensitive to radiation. (Organ at risk + internal margin +
setup margin)

Section 4:  Create a RapidArc™ Plan
You are now ready to create a RapidArc™ Plan.  Because your plan will be entering from multiple angles,
it is important to consider the couch and anything else that may be in the path of the beam. You want to
be sure you select the appropriate couch top that is being used for the plan you are creating. Eclipse
offers multiple selections to choose from.
Let’s begin by inserting a couch structure.
Insert Couch Structures
If treatment fields enter through treatment couch structures, the couch will attenuate some of the
treatment beam.  This attenuation may cause loss of skin-sparing effect for the patient. With IMRT and
RapidArc, the monitor units may be increased compared to conventional 3D planning. The increased
monitor units may decrease patient skin-sparing.
Insert couch structures when using IMRT or RapidArc techniques.
Before continuing, read the following Notes regarding Couch Structures.

Note: When inserting couch structures in an image:
Make sure that you have selected the correct couch profile, and that the couch structures are
positioned correctly. Visually verify the correctness of couch structures in each image plane.
If the 3D image is too small to fit the couch structures and you choose not to enlarge the
image, some couch structures might not be created completely. This can cause inaccuracies
in dose calculation.
Before using a couch structure for actual treatment, verify the HU values and resulting dose
distribution by using a phantom.
Reference: Eclipse Photon and Electron Instructions for Use. P1047678-002-B.

Reference: ExactIGRTCouchspec_RAD2661B_01/2009

Note: A collimator angle should be used with RapidArc plan to reduce the Tongue and
Groove effect in the plane of rotation. When the collimator is rotated slightly (example: 10 to
15 degrees, the tongue and groove effect does not cumulate in one plan of rotation. Using a
collimator angle can help cover larger parts of the target by using a diagonal distance.
Collimator Angle Information: Fixed: Fixed collimator angle in set degrees. Select Use
Complement Angle checkbox to add complement angles to the selected collimator angle.
A different collimator angle value may be entered. Automatic: The tool automatically
generates the collimator angles for the selected arc field. For multiple arc fields, the
collimator angles are varied from arc to arc, which can improve the special definition of the
beam, because the MLC modulates each arc field from a different direction. This option is
selected by default. Reference Eclipse Photon and Electron Instructions for Use.
P1047678-002-B, Pages 177-178.

Note: Collimator Angle Information: Fixed: Fixed collimator angle in set degrees. Select
Use Complement Angle checkbox to add complement angles to the selected collimator
angle. A different collimator angle value may be entered.
Automatic: The tool automatically generates the collimator angles for the selected arc field.
For multiple arc fields, the collimator angles are varied from arc to arc, which can improve
the special definition of the beam, because the MLC modulates each arc field from a
different direction. This option is selected by default. Reference Eclipse Photon and
Electron Instructions for Use. P1047678-002-B.

Note: Two or three isocenters may be used for very large or long fields to achieve target
coverage.  You can specify to treat the first arc clockwise or counterclockwise for a multiple
arc rotation.  If an isocenter shift is required, the user can specify if the first arc will be the
most superior or inferior field.

Section 5: Set Optimizer Settings for VMAT Planning
The Photon Optimizer algorithm has general, IMRT-specific and VMAT-specific calculation options.

1) Select the Calculation Models tab from the Information window.
a) Click Edit for the Photon Optimizer under the Calculation Options column.

2) The Calculation Options dialog box opens.
a) Select Optimizer Settings from the column on the left.
b) Inhomogeneity correction defines whether tissue heterogeneity correction is applied during
optimization. Ensure that this option is ON.
c) Air Cavity Correction defines whether a second order tissue heterogeneity correction is applied
during optimization. It is useful for cases where an air-tissue interface exists near the target. When
the air cavity correction option is used, the dose in tan air cavity is smaller than without this option.
d) Dose Calculation Resolution defines the grid resolution (in cm) for the optimizer’s internal dose
calculation engine. Available options are Normal (0.25 cm) and High (0.25 cm).
e) Dose Calculation Resolution for SRS or HyperArc defines the grid resolution (in cm) for the
optimizer’s internal dose calculation engine when the plan techniques is SRS or HyperArc. Options
are Normal (0.25 cm) and High (0.125 cm).
f)
Target Projection Margin defines the margin width of target projections that determine the target
masks. MLC apertures created by the VMAT optimizer and optimal fluences created by the IMRT
optimizer are restricted to these masks. Available options are Normal (6 mm) and Small (√2 ×the grid
resolution of the optimizer’s dose calculation engine. That is 3.5 mm or 1.8 mm).
g) Use GPU uses the graphics processing unit to accelerate the IMRT or VMAT optimization.

d
d
a

<!-- page 43 -->

3) Select VMAT options from the column on the left.
a) Convergence Mode defines how strict a convergence criterion is used in the optimization. The
available options are Extended, On and Off.
b) MR Level at Restart defines the multi-resolution level at which VMAT optimization restarts. Available
options are MR3 and MR4. The algorithm is allowed to increase MU still on MR3, but not at MR4 (with
or without convergence mode.
c) Aperture Shape Controller defines the strength of the aperture shape controller. It is a component of
the leaf sequencer for VMAT in the PO algorithm. ASC attempts to join disconnected apertures
defined by adjacent leaf pairs and may lower the number of monitor units and minimize QA errors.

Note: For more information see Photon and Electron Algorithms Reference Guide
P1047677-001-Rev-A, pages 173-174.

Section 6: Define RapidArc™ Optimization Parameters
Eclipse recognizes the type of optimization needed, either IMRT or VMAT by the type of fields that have
been created.
1) From the Planning Menu select Optimization>Optimize.

a) The Optimization dialog box opens.
b) The left side of the screen is where the Optimization objectives and parameters will be entered.
c) The right side of the screen has a DVH and patient image.

b
c
a
1

<!-- page 45 -->

Following are the different types of optimization objectives used in RapidArc™:
 Lower objectives: Any structure with a lower dose is considered a target. Lower dose ensures that
the volume receives a minimum dose.
 Upper objectives: Limit the dose to any structure and are used for targets as well as critical
structures.
 Mean objectives: Are used to decrease the dose that a structure receives (such as a percentage
dose to lung or parotid).
 gEUD objectives: gEUD objectives; Generalized Equivalent Uniform Dose (gEUD) is a uniform dose
that, if delivered over the same number of fractions, yields the same radiobiological effect as the non-
uniform dose distribution of interest. Structures with Lower gEUD and Target gEUD objectives are
considered to be targets.

Objectives:
Objectives are utilized to define the dose goals for a particular structure.
 Objectives are the dose and volume parameters that are considered optimal for the current patient
and plan.
Objectives can be added manually in the Structures and Objectives section, interactively by placing them
on the desired location within DVH, or from an Objective template.
Regardless of the method used to place objectives, they can all be viewed and modified in the tabular
area of Structures and Objectives as well as on the DVH.

<!-- page 46 -->

Upper objectives:
 Upper objectives are used for target and critical structures.
 Allows a maximum dose per unit volume. Example: 50% of RECTUM may receive 4700cGy, 50% of
BLADDER may receive 4500cGy, and 0% of the PTVpros SV marg 7673cGy.
 Numerous upper objectives can be defined per structure.  They can be added as needed during
optimization.
 Upper objectives can be modified during optimization.

<!-- page 47 -->

Lower objectives:
 Lower objectives are used only for target structures as it is a minimum dose request. Example: 100%
of PTVpros SV mar to receive 7486 cGy.
 Lower objectives are used to determine the treated area; therefore, they cannot be added after
optimization has begun to a structure which did not have a lower objective initially.
 Because the cost function is not symmetric around the target, each dose volume objective introduces
discontinuities in the optimization space and each additional objective added to the same structure
may create multiple new local minima. Therefore, it is recommended that only one lower objective be
defined per target structure.

Note: For more information see Photon and Electron Algorithms Reference Guide
P1047677-001-A.

Important Note: All objectives, doses and priorities are for demonstration purposes only
and are not meant to be recommendations by Varian or its employees.

<!-- page 48 -->

Priorities:
Priority must be assigned to each objective, indicating the structure’s relative importance in the plan.
Objectives within Eclipse range from 0-1000. These values are all relative to each other. This includes
priorities placed on smoothing and minimize dose in optimization.  From 0-100 is demonstrated in this
manual.
Priorities can be modified at any time before or during optimization in IMRT or RapidArc plans.
When adding an objective to any structure, the default priority is ‘0’. Change the priorities as necessary.
One method commonly used to help achieve target coverage is to place higher priorities on the targets
and lower priorities on the critical structures in IMRT Optimization.  After target coverage is achieved,
gradually increase critical structure priorities.

Note: The default Priority is “0”.  In order for Eclipse to optimize a structure, its priority
must be greater than “0”

<!-- page 49 -->

Volumes:
The structure volumes in cm³ will be displayed in full volume as well as corresponding to the Volume
percentage as objectives are added.
Eclipse randomly adds numbers for the Volume (%) and Dose to structures that are not the target of the
plan. Bladder full volume = 260.2m³; 50% of Bladder = 130.1cm³ in this example.

<!-- page 50 -->

Normal Tissue Objective (NTO)
The NTO default Priority setting is 100 for IMRT and RapidArc.
 All priorities, for critical structures, targets or the NTO, are relative to each other.  Since the range for
priorities is from 0-1000 for targets and critical structures for IMRT, it is important to remember this
relativity.
 Depending on the treatment area, for RapidArc™, the NTO may be less than, equal to, or
occasionally greater than the priorities of the targets and critical structures.
 The Normal Tissue Objective (NTO) is used to limit the dose and prevents hot spots in healthy tissue.
In addition, the NTO is used for obtaining a sharp dose gradient around the target volume. An NTO is
calculated for each target structure.
 Modifying these parameters will display the corresponding dose fall off in graphical format.
Automatic Normal Tissue Objective in PO
 The system defaults to automatically setting the Normal Tissue Objectives for you.  The Automatic
Normal Tissue Objective uses a set of internal parameters, so the parameters in the Normal Tissue
Objectives dialog box have no effect on it. The internal parameters depend on the distances of the
high dose areas from the target, and they are adapted dynamically so that they are suitable for the
patient anatomy and the objectives during the optimization. The Automatic Normal Tissue Objective
monitors the area within a certain distance around the target, and if there are doses that are
exceptionally high considering the distance from the target, it tries to reduce the dose at the area
using the user-defined priority value. (From Eclipse Photon and Electron Algorithms Reference Guide;
P1047677-001-A.)

<!-- page 51 -->

To Manually Define Parameters for the Normal Tissue Objective
 You may want to manually modify the Normal Tissue Objective.
 Distance from target border (cm) defines the area where the system begins evaluating the dose fall
off.
 Start dose (%) determines the relative dose level at the start of the NTO, in the area closest to the
target border. If there are multiple upper objectives on a target, the system uses the lowest upper
objective to calculate the start dose.
 End dose (%) defines the isodose level in the NTO furthest away from the target border.
 Fall-off determines the steepness of the NTO curve shape. Modifying the value in fall off will
essentially change the position of the end dose, closer to or further away from the PTV. It is helpful to
view the position of the end dose in centimeters on the graph.
 Modifying these parameters will display the corresponding dose fall off in graphical format.
 To manually set the NTO, deselect the Automatic NTO radio button and adjust the parameters.

<!-- page 52 -->

Exclude Structures from Optimization
Excluded structures are removed from the Structures and Objectives List.  Optimization DVHs cannot be
visualized on excluded structures.  Objectives cannot be added during optimization on excluded
structures.
1) To Exclude a structure:
a) Click the Excludes Structures from Optimization icon.

b) The Exclude Structures from Optimization dialog box opens.
c) From the Structures in Optimization section select Bones.
d) Select the right-facing arrow to move it to the Structures Excluded from Optimization section.
e) Click OK.

Note: If you want to return the structure to be included in the Optimization, reverse these
steps.

Section 7: Optimize Plan
The method for RapidArc™ Optimization that will be used in the procedures in this manual will be to first
work on target coverage and then work on critical structure sparing.  This is accomplished with higher
priorities placed on the target structures and lower priorities on the critical structures.  Once target
coverage is accomplished, the critical structures’ volumes, priorities and/or doses will be changed.
1) To begin optimizing:
a) Do not select either of the Automatic options for this plan.
b) Click Start VMAT Optimization.

c) The Optimizer starts.

in MR Level 1. To allow time to make changes during optimization, select the Pause button.

To change the target and/or critical structure priorities and/or dose, type the new values in the
appropriate boxes.  For this example, we want to improve on the sparing of the RectumSubPTV.
Therefore, change the priority to 65 for each Upper Objective of the RectumSubPTV.

Note: Optimization will continue but the system will not continue to the next MR level.

g) To continue through the steps, click the Play button.

Improving your Optimization results
As the optimizer works, you may want to continue to improve on the results.
Once the Optimizer has met a structure’s objectives the Optimizer will not work on that structure. In this
example, the BLADDER’s objectives have been met.
1) To make the Optimizer work on any structures that are no longer being optimized, you may drag an
objective below the DVH line; change the Priority or the Dose in the structure list.

Note: All changes should be slight at first so as not to disrupt the target coverage.

watch the real-time DVH and isodose line results.

<!-- page 77 -->

Intermediate Dose Calculation
1) Once you are satisfied with the optimization, Eclipse can calculate an intermediate dose, which
improves the agreement between the final dose DVH calculation and the DVH calculated during
optimization with the MRDC algorithm.

Note: To improve optimization results, you can calculate an intermediate dose during the
IMRT or RapidArc optimization.  This is useful especailly for heterogeneous targets, such
as lungs.  The intermediate dose is calculated from the current fluences in the plan, using
the same dose calculation algorithm as in the final dose calculation.
Automatic Intermediate Dose is set by default. In addition, when re-optimizing a plan that
already has a dose distribution, you can use the existing dose as an intermediate dose
during the optimization.
a) Select Automatic Intermediate Dose.
b) Click Intermediate Dose.

Note: All other functions are disabled during the intermediate dose calculation.

Note: After the optimization completes, you return to External Beam Planning to calculate

Section 9: RapidArc™ Head and Neck with Supraclavicular Nodes
Head and neck planning can be challenging whether you are planning 3D, IMRT or RapidArc™.  One of the
challenges are the many overlapping structures that are important to treat the patient.
In some cases, additional (partial) structures may be created to provide the optimizer with criteria that may
help achieve the overall clinical goals.  For example, if two structure overlap, the structure that is “least
important” can be subtracted from the more important structure.  This will allow you to adjust the priorities to
achieve your goals. (Goals are on page 117).

In the case of multiple concentric targets receiving different doses, an unconstrained area can be created
to encourage the optimizer to modulate the dose.
With RapidArc™, an unconstrained area between targets may yield hot or cold spots in this area.
Because no constraints are placed here, the Normal Tissue Objective (NTO) may also take effect in this
area.
To help the Manual NTO avoid modifying the dose between the targets and to place constraints for the
optimizer, additional targets may be created.

Note: This technique may not be necessary with the Automatic NTO.

<!-- page 112 -->

Determining Avoidance Sectors in External Beam Planning
You can add avoidance sectors to your plan.  Avoidance sectors are ranges of the Gantry rotations where
no MUs are delivered.  The beam is turned off.
 Each avoidance sector length must be at least 15 degrees.
 Two avoidance sectors are permitted per arc.
 The minimum length for the Beam-on between two avoidance sectors or between the start/stop
angles and an avoidance sector must be at least 15 degrees.
 It is helpful to visualize any avoidance sectors in External Beam Planning before entering
Optimization.

Note: There are several options to consider for the shoulders when the supraclavicular
nodes are included in RapidArc™ head and neck fields.  Avoidance sector(s) can be
created; multiple arcs can be used to avoid the shoulders; humeral heads may be outlined
and given maximum upper dose objectives; non-coplanar beams may be utilized; or the
shoulders may at times be ignored for optimization and integral dose verified upon plan
approval. In this exercise, avoidance sectors will be created for the left and right humeral
heads for both arc fields.
1) To evaluate the sectors to avoid:
a) In External Beam Planning, select Insert.
b) Select New Field…

<!-- page 130 -->

Normal Tissue Objective (NTO)
The NTO is important in RapidArc™ plans to decrease/eliminate hot spots near the edge of the
target.
The NTO can be set to Automatic or created Manually for RapidArc™.  In most cases the Automatic
NTO is sufficient to provide steeper dose fall off as desired.  SRS cases may require the use of the
manual NTO.
1) To set the NTO parameters Manually:
a) Click the NTO Expander.
b) Click the Manual radio button to deselect Automatic NTO.

a
b

<!-- page 131 -->

c) The following are good starting points for manual NTO settings:
 Distance from target border of 0.2cm, will begin the penalty for dose in normal tissue to be
calculated at 2mm from the edge of the target.
 Start dose of 100% will not allow any more than the lowest upper objective of a given target
volume outside in normal tissue.
 Making the End dose % value too low will prohibit the optimizer from dosing the target.  Use End
dose of 60-70%
 Fall off: 0.1 – 0.15 will create a steep fall off and help to avoid hot spots where they are most
likely to occur.

c

<!-- page 132 -->

d) To use the Automatic NTO, select the Automatic NTO radio button.
 As in the manual NTO, the system will place a series of points within the patient (excluding target
tissue).
 It then compares dose to all points at a given distance away from the target and averages them.
 Any points above the average at the specific distance will receive a penalty; subsequently, the
dose will be decreased to those points.
 The system will group, average and compare points at each distance away from the target
together. Example: points 2mm away from the target will be averaged together; points 3mm away
from the target will be averaged together separately; and so on, to the periphery of the patient.
 The system will try to make the dose uniform outside the target at the specified distance to avoid
any fingers or streaks of dose coming from the arc directions that receive more dose.
 When the automatic NTO is utilized the parameters of the manual NTO are grayed our and
ignored.
 Very steep gradients required for SRS cases may still be better achieved with the manual NTO.

d
