Section 10: VMAT Head and Neck using Aperture Shape Controller
Aperture Shape Controller Overview
Aperture Shape Controller (ASC) is a new component in the leaf sequencer for VMAT in the PO
algorithm. ASC increases the size and decreases the complexity of the MLC aperture on average. ASC is
not available for non-interdigitating MLCs. ASC is limited to Varian/TrueBeam/Edge machines only and is
not available for the Halcyon and 3rd Party MLCs at present.
Controlling the size and shape of the apertures may help reduce; QA failures, dose delivery inaccuracies,
dependency on MLC modeling and positioning, and MU.
ASC favors apertures of minimal local curvature by penalizing deviations from zero curvature as
measured based on the positions of the tips of adjacent leaves that modulate the same spatially
continuous target projection.
The incurred penalty is added to the cost function of the optimization. The magnitude of the penalty can
be controlled in part by the user-adjustable weight via a calculation option from Very Low to Very High,
whereby the shapes of VMAT apertures can be tuned to meet the requirements of each clinical case.
Treatments that require a high level of leaf modulation, such as head and neck treatments, should
accommodate a weight from Very Low up to the Moderate setting.
Depending on the weight, ASC attempts to join disconnected apertures defined by adjacent leaf pairs.
Cases in which this can be carried out without significantly violating dosimetric optimization objectives,
may exhibit a lower number of monitor units than when planned without ASC.

Section 11: Auto-Feathering and Multiple Energies
Auto-feathering Overview
PO algorithm now contains automatic feathering that creates smooth dose gradients in plans with multiple
isocenters where the treatment fields overlap. This eliminates the need to do manual match line shifts on
the treatment machine or optimize with low dose gradient structures. It works with both VMAT and IMRT
and for all VARIAN Linacs including Halcyon.
Automatic feathering functionality strives to reduce the error caused by patient position inaccuracies by
adding a set of spatial optimization objectives to the objective set of the plan in the preprocessing step of
the optimization algorithm. The Photon Optimizer has a feature that allows the algorithm to feather the
dose to long volumes that have two or more isocenter groups.
The fields are overlapped by 5cm to 10cm or greater at the junction to allow the optimizer algorithm to
feather the dose. The dose in the 2 fields’ overlap region is made a uniform dose by the PO algorithm.
The PO will cause the dose to smoothly ramp up and ramp down in the overlap. The algorithm can reduce
treatment inaccuracies, eliminates manual matching of abutting fields and the need for junction shifts
every few days.

Optimize with Auto-Feathering and Multiple Energies
The Photon Optimizer algorithm now supports optimizing a multi-energy dose calculation with any
combination of energy modes for both IMRT and RapidArc fields. Field dose calculation will be used if Acuros
XB is used for calculating dose for multi-energy plans. If Plan Dose is selected as On in the Calculation
Models Options, the setting will automatically be turned Off for multi-energy plans.
There are no additional settings in the Optimization window for Auto-Feathering. Once Auto-Feathering is
turned on in the Calculation Options, Auto-feathering is used for multiple isocenter plans if the set Z Threshold
is exceeded. The algorithm divides the treatment fields into field groups according to their isocenter position in
the Z direction. For each group, the volume that is visible from the fields of the group is calculated and stored
in a spatial grid of voxels. The grids for all groups are then analyzed for overlap regions. Upper dose spatial
optimization objectives, one per field group, are created if field groups overlap.

During optimization, the dose arising from the fields of each group is stored separately, the value of the spatial
objective for that group is evaluated, and the value added to the total sum of objective cost. The gradient of
the cost function is likewise evaluated separately for each field group, so that the gradient for a field group
contains the components arising from all the user defined objectives plus the field group specific component.
In general, the smoothing effect of feathering increases as size of the overlap region increases, because the
slope of the dose gradient becomes less sharp. It is therefore recommended to use as large overlap area as
possible.

The memory on the current GPUs is limited to 16GB RAM. If very small grid sizes, or large fields, are used
with very large CT data sets, or the auto feathering tool in optimization, the dose calculation or optimization
will run out of memory. In such a case, it is advised to use either the CPU calculation for Acuros XB or PO
IMRT, or to use a larger calculation grid size. If multiple fields are used, it can help to switch to Acuros XB to
field dose calculation, which typically uses less memory than the plan dose calculation. For more information,
please see Eclipse Photon and Electron Algorithms Reference Guide.

Tip1: Auto feathering uses the same internal priority as the highest target lower objective
in the plan. If the dose in the overlap region is not clinically optimal (higher or lower than
desired), you can manually adjust the strength of the auto feathering spatial objective by
adding an additional lower dose objective at 0.1 Gy higher than the highest existing lower
objective. Start with a priority ~50% less (i.e. half) to increase the dose in the overlap
region and then adjust as needed. To reduce the dose in the overlap region, increase the
priority by 50% (i.e. 1.5 times) and then adjust as needed

Tip2: If you are optimizing on one PTV with more than two isocenters and if one of the
gradients in the overlap region is good and the gradient in the other overlap region is not
acceptable, we recommend splitting the PTV at or close to the junction of the second
isocenter and optimize on two PTV volumes.  This will give the auto feathering feature
more flexibility to control the gradient per each overlap individually.  Tip1 can also be
combined with this tip

<!-- page 184 -->

Tip3: If you are optimizing on a IMRT plan with more than one isocenter with a X = or > 30,
and a collimator angle of 90 or 270, which will result in a split carriage plan, the plan will fail
to calculate if the jaws are not locked during optimization.

ramp up and ramp down from the contributing fields. The Dose Profile will look like an X at the center
of the histogram with a single straight line representing all fields as shown in Figure 1.

Section 12: Multi-Criteria Optimization (MCO) Based Trade-Off Exploration
Multi-Criteria Optimization (MCO) -based Trade-Off Exploration offers tools for real-time exploration and
visual evaluation of the range of trade-offs in target coverage and healthy tissue sparing for IMRT, VMAT
and HyperArc plans.
The trade-offs between selected optimization objectives are modeled by a collection of plan alternatives
(or variations) that is generated with a multi-criteria optimization algorithm in Eclipse. The algorithm
creates 3N+1 alternative plans (single target) and 3N+2 (multiple targets) plans for every objective
selected. You can explore the trade-offs between the objectives in the Optimization dialog box, view the
effects of the modifications you make in the DVH View, and changes in the dose displayed in the 2D
View. Actual dose values display for the optimization objectives and in the Clinical Goals tab (if the plan
has a clinical protocol reference with defined Clinical Goals).
New to v16.0 and higher, Multi-Criteria Optimization-based Trade-Off Exploration contain the following:
 If the plan contains clinical goals, they are added into the algorithm as constraint functions. The
selection and restriction mechanisms can then be used to explore the plan space via clinical goals.
 After trade-off exploration, you can perform local dose correction on the calculated dose distribution
of the deliverable IMRT or VMAT plan.

Note: Target Autocrop, ALDO and excluding fields from optimization are not supported
when you use Trade-Off Exploration.

Concepts Related to Multi-Criteria Optimization Trade-Off Exploration:
Trade-off Exploration: Improving a trade-off objective and at the same time letting one or more of the
other trade-off objectives degrade, if that is required for improving the selected objective.
Trade-off Objective: Optimization objective selected for trade-off exploration.
Plan collection: The plan collection consists of a number of plans, including the balanced plan and all
alternative plans, which are used for exploring trade-offs between the trade-off objectives.
Balanced plan: During the plan collection generation, a copy of the original plan is taken, and it is re-
optimized with the original optimization objectives. This plan is called the balanced plan and it is used as a
seed for subsequent steps of the plan collection generation.
Pareto surface: The Pareto surface is a concept in multi-criteria optimization. Any treatment plan in the
plan collection balances the different trade-offs regarding tumor coverage and sparing of OARs in a
certain way. The entirety of treatment plans where it is not possible to improve one objective without
degradation of another objective, with respect to certain, fixed trade-off objectives is called the Pareto
surface.
Deliverable plan: Deliverable plan is created by converting the trade-off exploration result into a plan in
which the limitations of the treatment unit are taken into account.

<!-- page 193 -->

Local Dose Correction: Improving trade-off exploration result by selecting a point in the dose around
which you want to increase or decrease the dose.
Locally Dose Corrected Plan: Locally Dose Corrected Plan is created by optimizing a new plan based
on the trade-off exploration result and then using local dose correction to increase or decrease dose
around a point in the dose distribution.
For more information on MCO based Trade-off exploration, please refer to P1047677-001-A Eclipse
Photon and Electron Algorithm Reference guide and the P1047678-002-B Eclipse Photon and
Electron Instructions for Use

<!-- page 194 -->

Points to Consider About Using Trade-Off Exploration with IMRT/VMAT
Optimization:
 Target Autocrop and excluding fields from optimization are not supported when you use Trade-Off
Exploration.
 For plans with clinical goals, the Optimization dialog shows how well the clinical goals are met.
 For IMRT plans, intermediate dose can be calculated, or the current plan dose can be used as
intermediate dose when the plan collection for trade-off exploration is generated.
 If the dose from a base dose plan is a substantial fraction of prescribed dose, plan generation may
not produce clinically useful trade-off exploration range.
 Local Dose Correction is not supported if a base dose plan is used.
 You need to carefully verify the optimization result and the final dose distribution.

Multi-Criteria Optimization Trade-Off Exploration
The Trade-Off Exploration Mode of the Optimization dialog box opens after the plan collection has been
generated. The trade-offs between different optimization objectives are explored using the slider shown in the
dialog box, and the modifications made during the exploration are shown in real-time in the DVH View and the
2D View.
The optimization objectives are selected for trade-off exploration and are shown as sliders in the upper left
part of the dialog box. The optimization objectives from the original plan are shown below the sliders. You can
show the details of the objectives and show or hide structures. You can show objectives from the interactive
optimization or from the balance plan by selecting one of the options above the structure list. The optimization
objectives are read-only in the Trade-Off Exploration mode.
The DVH View and the 2D views are updated in real time when you explore the trade-offs. The optimization
objectives cannot be modified in the DVH View, but you can scale, zoom, pan and reset the zoom and pan of
the DVH View. Structures can be shown in the DVH by selecting them from the list. You can select the type of
reference DVH shown in the DVH View.
For the 2D View, you have the same image visualization tools available as for interactive optimization in the
Optimization dialog box. The type of dose currently shown is indicated above the 2D View.
The Clinical Goals tab shows the Clinical Goals for plans that have them defined. Clinical Goals are
interactive as you explore trade-offs. There are check box options on this pane to automatically explore the
possibilities of modifying the current trade-off exploration range so that the clinical goals can be fulfilled. After
the trade-off collection is created, you can first use these tools to explore the clinical goals, and then continue
exploring the trade-offs using the sliders. There are two options available to restrict clinical goals:
•
Fulfilling all clinical goals at once: Click Restrict on the Clinical Goals tab to modify the available
exploration range so that all of the clinical goals are fulfilled within that range. If all the clinical goals
cannot be fulfilled, the tool modifies the exploration range so that as many as possible of the clinical
goals, in the priority order, are fulfilled.
•
Fulfilling the goals one at a time: For an individual goal, select the check box of the clinical goal on the
Clinical Goal pane to modify the available exploration range so that the clinical goal in question is
fulfilled within that range.
To release the exploration range restriction, clear the check boxes. The check box is only shown for those
clinical goals that can be fulfilled within the available exploration range.

Local Dose Correction Tool
New to v16.0 and higher versions, the Local Dose Correction tool can improve dose at selected points in the
plan. After Multi-Criteria Optimization Trade-Offs are made, select the Local Dose Correction mode. The
system will calculate the dose for the explored plan. You can only mark one point for correction. If you are not
satisfied with the result, you can discard the results and mark a different point for correction.

The Focus on Structure tool now has new selections to help in locating hot spots or cold spots in the plan that
you may want to correct. Select a structure from the optimization structures list. To locate the hottest spots in
the structure, select Focus on Structure Maximum Dose. For the coldest spots in the structure, select Focus
on Structure Minimum Dose. The viewing plane intersection is moved to the dose point in the structure.
Continue to browse through the structures, and the image view is updated according to the selection made
from the Focus on Structure tool.

When the viewing plane intersection is at the location of the dose point you want to correct, click Decrease
Dose or Increase Dose. The generation of the corrected plan starts immediately.

Note the following about marking a point for dose correction:
 Markers for increasing dose cannot be added further than a defined margin, 5mm from an
optimization target structure.
 The minimum dose line for the area where a point can be marked for correction is 5% of the
maximum dose.
 The viewfinder is shown with a dashed line in locations where points cannot be marked for correction.

When the marker has been added, the viewfinder is no longer shown, and the marker is shown in the image
view. The current position of the marker and the dose at the marker are shown under Local Dose Correction,
as well as in the tool tip of the marker. Once the plan has been created, the slider for exploring the plan is
shown under Local Dose Correction. Move the local dose correction slider to the left to increase or decrease
the dose, depending on the selection type, at the point of interest and in the volume surrounding it. You can
see the effect of the modifications you make immediately in the DVH View, 2D View and Clinical Goals tab.

Tip: To continue trade-off exploration and discard the local dose correction results, click
Trade-Off Exploration. If you want to discard the current Local Dose Correction results, but
continue working with Local Dose Correction, click Discard Results.

Section 13: Planning with RapidPlan Models
RapidPlan is a treatment planning tool that allows clinics to use the dose and patient anatomy information
from previous plans, in the form of DVH estimation models, to estimate the dose distribution in new plans
created in optimization. In this way, the DVH estimation models are representative of their clinical
practice. The clinic may also use a Varian provided DVH Estimation model. Varian provided DVH
Estimation models are representative of a clinical site that Varian has collaborated with such as
Washington University, St. Louis or University of Alabama, Birmingham.
New to v16.0 and higher, the DVH Estimation algorithm for RapidPlan now supports multiple target dose
levels and multiple isocenters in a plan.
Introduction to RapidPlan
With RapidPlan, a DVH estimation model generates an estimated DVH range that shows where the DVH
curve of a structure will most likely land based on the data set collection. You can generate DVH
estimates for IMRT or VMAT plans. The DVH estimation models are available in the Optimization dialog
box. To be able to use RapidPlan DVH estimates, you will need to optimize the IMRT or VMAT plan with
the PO algorithm.

Note: RapidPlan knowledge-based planning and its models are not intended to replace
clinical decisions, provide medical advice or endorse any particular radiation plan or
treatment procedure. The patient’s medical professionals are solely responsible for and
must rely on their professional clinical judgment when deciding how to plan and provide
radiation therapy.

<!-- page 218 -->

The DVH estimation model uses knowledge from existing treatment plans to generate DVH estimates and
estimate-based optimization objectives in optimization. The data from the existing treatment plans is
extracted and then used to train the DVH estimation models. DVH estimation models are configured,
trained and managed in the DVH Estimation Model Configuration workspace. The user should note that
RapidPlan knowledge-based planning and the DVH estimation models are used as a starting point for
planning as an aid in creating consistent plans in the clinic based on previous planning results.

Selecting Plans to Train for RapidPlan
The DVH estimation algorithm is used for creating and applying DVH estimation models to be used in
treatment planning with RapidPlan. The main purpose of the DVH estimation algorithm is to estimate what
would be an achievable Dose-Volume Histogram for various critical organs. The estimations are based on
the extracted data from actual DVHs achieved in earlier planned patient cases. These estimated DVHs
can be translated into optimization objectives. The optimization objectives are set so that the optimizer
tries to achieve the estimated DVHs from the model.

<!-- page 219 -->

RapidPlan Model Configuration
The algorithm is divided into the model configuration and the DVH estimation components. The model
configuration component is used for configuring new DVH estimation models that can then be used in the
DVH estimation component in the optimizer for the new plan. The model configuration component
consists of two phases, the data extraction phase and the model training phase. The DVH estimation
component consists of estimation generation and objective generation.

The plans chosen for data extraction and model training should be good plans that are representative of
the clinic’s normal planning process. It is good practice to use the same naming scheme for the target and
organ at risk structures. This will ensure less work during the data extraction phase and then later when
applying a DVH Estimation Model in optimization for the new plan. The new plan, then, will be based on
the achievable DVH from the extracted and trained library of plans. For the best results, the training set
should have a wide variety of:
 Body sizes and shapes.
 OAR sizes and shapes.
 Target sizes and shapes.
 Field geometry.
 Planning techniques.
This will ensure a good fit of plans for future plans.

<!-- page 220 -->

The structure set uses a segment model for representing each of the structures. The segment model
determines the location of the structure surface. All the metrics calculated by the DVH estimation
algorithm are voxel-based and use the same resolution for the representation of the structure as it is used
in the Eclipse client. Each voxel has a value from 0.0 to 1.0. The value represents the fraction of the voxel
volume that belongs to the structure. Training is based on voxel volume.

The model training phase of the DVH estimation algorithm is used for creating DVH estimation models.
The model training phase produces a DVH estimation model, which is actually a set of DVH estimation
models of separate Organs-at-Risk. The DVH estimation model for each OAR is also composed as a set
of DVH estimation models; one model per OAR region. Each OAR region model is calculated separately
using the same steps. Outliers are removed to produce the final DVH estimate

<!-- page 221 -->

Targets and Target Levels
RapidPlan now supports a maximum of 10 model targets and multiple target dose levels. This may be
useful for complex plans that have more than 2 or 3 targets. RapidPlan models with one target level may
be used for plans with multiple target dose levels and the target dose levels can also be defined in
optimization. This is significant since model plan statistics are accumulated faster with fewer model
targets potentially decreasing the need for a larger training set.

You can configure a DVH estimation model with only one target dose level, and still use it for plans with
multiple target dose levels. The plan targets can be matched to the single model target, and the target
dose levels can be defined in optimization.
Additional model targets only need to be created if unique target objectives are desired per target
dose level. Unique model targets only need to be created where the target dose distributions are
qualitatively different such as in a simultaneous integrated boost.
DVH Estimation Algorithm
The DVH estimation algorithm extracts the target dose values for all the target structures of the plan
and are mapped to a target in the Rapid Plan model. When a new plan is added to the model, data is
extracted. The target dose values for all target structures are mapped to a target in the model. When
adding a new plan to a model, the plan target structures get the plan prescription dose as the default
target dose value, but only if the plan target is automatically mapped to a target in the model. If the
target is not mapped, the dose value will be empty and must be defined by the user.

<!-- page 222 -->

New Line Objective Types
In v15.5 and higher, the user can now select either a target preferring line objective or an OAR
preferring line objective. In previous versions, if an OAR overlapped a target structure, the line objective
always preferred the target in the overlap region. Now, the user can define the new OAR preferring line
objective which favors the OAR in the overlap region. The selection is made in Model Configuration

Improved OAR-Target Overlap Modeling
The Overlapping OAR-Target handling has been improved in versions 15.6 and higher. The joint
overlapping DVH refers to the DVH of the part in which the OAR is overlapping with any of the model’s
targets. In previous versions of the DVH estimation algorithm, the overlap area estimate of an OAR with
the target was modeled by using a single extended model of the joint overlap DVH’s of the OAR. Single
statistics were collected for a particular model structure and the DVHs were scaled by the highest target
dose level. In cases when there are several model targets, this estimate was very wide and the same
estimates were given, no matter whether the overlap region was with the low-dose or high-dose target.
Now, the OAR-Target overlap region is split between the different target dose levels and each sub-region
is scaled by the target dose level in the overlapping region. Different statistics are then collected for each
model target to improve results.

<!-- page 223 -->

Small Structure Handling
Small structure handling is better supported in versions 15.5 and higher. The internal structure
representation has switched from volumetric to the segment model. During the extraction phase in older
versions, the algorithm would skip any structure that was half of a voxel or less in size. Any skipped
structure was not used for training the models nor recorded. The DVH estimation may not have been
possible or not successful for very small structures in the past.
Now, all the voxels of the structure including those in which the structure occupies less than half a voxel
are considered in the calculation instead of scaling everything to 2.5 mm. The resolution can now be set
by the user. In this way, every voxel belonging even partially to the structure is contributing to the DVH
calculation. The DVH sampling is based on the centroid of the volume belonging to the structure. And,
since Geometry-based Expected Dose calculation depends on structure resolution DVH and GEDVH
calculation is always successful.

<!-- page 224 -->

Principal Component Analysis
The Principal component analysis during the training phase of the model has improved. DVH and
geometrically expected DVH parametrization is based on principal component analysis. The RapidPlan
algorithm finds curves that represent the variation of individual DVHs compared to the mean curve shown
by the yellow dotted line. For small structures, typical DVHs are close to step functions requiring multiple
principal components to present variable locations of the step. Therefore, presenting variations from the
mean curve requires multiple PC’s. Since DVHs and cumulative volume histograms for the geometrical
expected dose are monotonically decreasing functions, they can be replaced by their inverse functions;
functions of volume rather than dose. Performing principal component analysis for Volume Dose
Histograms (VDHs) can lead to a single PC that can explain most of the variation.

In practice, principal component analysis based on inverse DVHs is beneficial. Small structures having
step function type DVHs typically benefit when the principal components analysis is made based on
inverse DVHs. Also, it is beneficial for structures where the DVHs hit zero in different locations as
depicted here by the red dotted lines on the cumulative VDH on top and the DVH on the bottom. The
artificial long foot in the estimates of the quickly falling curve is also reduced using the method of principal
component analysis.

<!-- page 225 -->

DVH Estimation Component
The DVH Estimation component of the DVH estimation algorithm is used for generating estimated DVHs
and optimization objectives for a plan. As input, the DVH estimation component takes a trained DVH
estimation model, a structure set from the plans and the dose levels of targets. The DVH estimation
algorithm outputs a set of DVHs representing upper and lower boundary estimates for each of the
supported OAR structures and a set of optimization objectives. The optimization objectives are set so that
the optimizer tries to achieve the estimated DVHs.
During optimization, the line objective drives the current DVH towards the set objective. Line objectives
pull all parts of the DVH curve with the same strength, fitting the DVH curve to a profile similar to one part
of the objective line. The Line Objective is displayed below the DVH curve modeling band. If part of the
objective line is below the plan’s DVH curve, that part of the line has no effect on the DVH curve shape.
Only those line parts that are located above the plan’s DVH curve have a pulling effect on the curve. This
type of constraint guarantees that if optimization finds the solution fulfilling the estimates, it also minimizes
the cost function.
All OAR objective priorities are given relative to the priorities of the target objectives. As an additional
condition, the line objective has the same shape as the lower estimates, so that it can be below the lower
bound at every dose-volume if the same shift is used. Thus, the line objective has constant shift to the left
from the lower estimate, that is, the shift is the same at every volume percentage. During optimization, the
line objective will drive the Plan DVH into the model estimate band.

If a target-preferring line objective is used, the line objective is modified in the high dose volume, which
corresponds to the overlap volumes of different target structures. Steps will be generated to the line
objective so that it does not conflict with the lower objectives of the target. The target objectives are
preserved with this modification. Models must be validated before clinical use.

Tips for Using DVH Estimates in Planning
Selecting a DVH Estimation Model for a Plan:
 The treatment site must be the same that the DVH estimation model has been configured, trained
and validated for.
 The number of Targets does not have to be the same.
 The generated DVH estimates depend on the field geometry that you define for a plan. If the defined
field geometry is not optimal, the generated DVH estimates may also be suboptimal.
 The structures must be contoured according to the same guidelines as the structures in the plans
that were used to train and validate the DVH estimation model. Depending on the used structure
resolution in contouring, the generated DVH estimates may differ slightly.
 Fractionation for the plan should match the DVH estimation model.
 Total dose should match the DVH estimation model.
 When you copy a plan, the DVH Estimates are also copied.
 DVH Estimates are included in the DVH printout.
Optimizing a Plan with RapidPlan Models:
 If you want to use dose objectives from a model together with dose objective you enter yourself, add
the objectives from the model first, as adding them later will override all manually entered dose
objectives.
 If your plan contains multiple targets, you can match them to the same model target, if the dose
distributions of targets are similar and the same model-generated optimization objectives are
suitable for all the targets.
 Dose objectives from a template cannot be used in the same plan as dose objectives from a DVH
estimation model.
 In HyperArc plans, the target dose levels are defined in the HyperArc dialog box, and you cannot
modify them in optimization.
 Trade-off Exploration can be used with DVH Estimation Models.
 A Base-Dose Plan can be used with DVH Estimation Models. Verify the optimization result and the
final dose calculation carefully.
 DVH estimates are removed if the field geometry is changed, a field is deleted, or a new structure
set is assigned to the plan.
 In VMAT plans, DVH estimates are also cleared when Avoidance Sectors are modified.
 DVH estimates are not cleared after the following modifications:
•
Resetting or modifying the calculation volume.
•
Changing the plan normalization mode.

<!-- page 244 -->

•
Changing the DVH estimation algorithm.
•
Editing the fluence map for IMRT plans.

Caution: Do not base treatment decisions only on the DVH estimations. DVH estimations
can be used in plan evaluation, but they alone do not indicate that the plan is clinically
acceptable. Evaluate the plan, including the dose distribution in 2D views and calculated
DVH, thoroughly before clinical use. In addition to DVH estimations, use all the other
necessary plan evaluation tools available.

<!-- page 245 -->

Section 14: HyperArc Cranial Stereotactic Treatment
Concept
HyperArc Stereotactic Treatment Technique is a is a non-coplanar VMAT based technique used for
cranial SRS treatments based on a fixed geometry setup that provides high plan quality for single
isocenter and single or multiple target cranial radiosurgery.
 The setup is based on a fixed geometry, up to 4 arcs with 3 of them non-coplanar arcs. Fewer
arcs are used if the case is less complex. This setup can be used for multiple targets as well as
for single target.
 With non-coplanar arcs, all potential delivery geometries around a patient's target volume can be
used to arrive at the optimal dose coverage, while providing the highest conformity and the most
sparing of normal tissue.
 Linked to this treatment technique is the concept of collision prevention, avoidance and detection.
 HyperArc enables automated treatment delivery on HyperArc capable treatment units, which
shortens the overall treatment time as you can irradiate multiple targets at the same time without
the need to re-position the patient, or to enter the treatment room to manually move the couch
between the arc fields.
Benefits
 Automation and Efficiency: achieved by making treatment planning as automated as possible with
limited user input. Placement of the isocenter is automated as is treatment delivery.
 Clinical Benefit: the goal is to deliver the dose to the target volumes as conformal and compact
as possible. Dose to brain tissue and other organs at risk are reduced and dose to target
volume will be higher. This will be achieved by improving the optimization process for VMAT
plans
 Safety: the goal is to deliver the SRS plans using a one-push button on the treatment unit, in a
safe manner. The treatment planning process will aim to create plans that are collision free and
will alert the user if there is a risk of collision between machine-machine or patient-machine. The
treatment planning process for HyperArc provides a collision check and the capability to perform
a visual Virtual Dry Run.
 Clinical Class Solution: By providing a clinical class solution that works, clinicians can implement
a complex treatment technique with confidence. The class solution provides high quality plans
and high quality and safe treatment delivery. It provides a Non-coplanar VMAT treatment
technique that can treat multiple cranial targets with a single isocenter and pre-planned geometry.

<!-- page 246 -->

Set Up
Set up if all 4 Arcs are used:
 1 full coplanar arc with couch rotation of 0 degrees.
 1 half non-coplanar arc with couch rotation of -45 degrees.
 1 half non-coplanar arc with couch rotation of +45degrees.
 1 half non-coplanar arc (vertex) arc with couch rotation of + or – 90degrees.
 The ENCOMPASS couch top from QFix will be used as the required fixation device for the
SRS cranial treatment. There are two types of ENCOMPASS fixation devices:

ENCOMPASS overlay board that will be fixed on top of an IGRT couch.

ENCOMPASS insert that will be inserted on a kVue couch.
 The field order is set automatically, and it cannot be changed. The field order is optimized to
minimize the treatment time within the defined operating limits. However, you can define the
setup field to be either the first or the last field.

<!-- page 247 -->

HyperArc Workflow
1) HyperArc treatment requires that the planning CT image contains the support structures that model the
Encompass fixation device used in the treatment. The CT image should include the patient’s shoulders
and the top of the Encompass device. The CT image should include the patient’s shoulders and the top of
the Encompass fixation device which will improve the dose calculation accuracy, virtual dry run and
alignment of the Encompass model against the CT-image. The CT-image should have a small slice
thickness: recommended 1.25mm or smaller. It is also common practice to have an MR image for
accurately contouring the targets
2) Import in the CT and MR images and perform a rigid registration
3) In contouring: insert the Encompass fixation model. The system will auto align the device. However, the
end user may manually align it with the CT image. Modify the Body contour to eliminate the Encompass
baseplate (procedure for this is included further on. Contour all targets and OARs and make them high
resolution structures.
4) In External Beam Planning, insert a HyperArc Plan and follow the plan creation wizard.
5) Check the Virtual Dry Run to check for potential collisions.
6) In Optimization, apply the RapidPlan Intra-Cranial Multimet Model (comes with the purchase of HyperArc)
or apply an objective template as desired. Continue with Optimization. Review the Target Quality Metrics
in Optimization.
7) Continue to Dose Calculation. Review the plan and the Target Quality Metrics.
8) Plan approve the plan.
9) In Treatment Preparation, schedule the imaging and treatment sessions and Treatment Approve the plan.

3) Select either the Encompass Overlay or the Encompass Insert. Only one fixation device can be

Encompass Device alignment
The Encompass fixation device used in HyperArc planning is added to the patient image as a support
structure model. The model includes two Encompass support structures and three alignment markers
(center, right, left). The Encompass structures cannot be added, modified, copied, or moved in External
Beam Planning. They can be deleted by going to the structure menu option and selecting Delete all
Encompass Structures. You can also duplicate a structure set with Encompass structures.
It is important that both the Overlay and the Insert have been aligned correctly against the couch top in
longitudinal direction. Otherwise, the fields may be outside of the patient protection zone in the
longitudinal direction and the automated delivery is thus prevented. Note that there is one model that
represents the Overlay and the Insert device. The difference is in how the two devices are fixated on the
couch.
The alignment is done automatically by the system upon insertion of the device using the three markers in
the CT Image. However, users may adjust the alignment manually by using the CT-image where the
Encompass fixation device is clearly visible. An extra check of the alignment can be done based on three
markers, called fixation alignment points that are imbedded in the Encompass fixation device. The
markers can be seen in the image below. The alignment of the Encompass fixation device to the CT-
image is a prerequisite to create a HyperArc plan in external beam planning as the markers are used as
information for the position of the patient on the treatment unit.
The alignment of the ENCOMPASS fixation device with patient CT-scan will give following result:

Patient Protection Zone
Patient protection zone is the area where no collision with the treatment unit is expected to occur in
HyperArc treatments. This applies to collisions between the patient and gantry, and between the couch
and gantry. The patient protection zone is defined by using the 95th percentile male patient size, and it is
based on the fixation device used in the treatment unit (Encompass IGRT Overlay and Encompass kVue
insert)
On the treatment unit, the patient protection zone defines the allowed limits for the couch position. The
limits apply after the patient has been positioned for the treatment, and any imaging-based couch
corrections have been made. The maximum allowed couch corrections for pitch, roll, and rotation of the
6DoF couch in HyperArc treatments is 3 degrees.
In treatment planning, the patient protection zone defines the allowed limits for the isocenter position. It
determines the volume, in which the isocenter of the plan can be placed after the imaging-based couch
corrections have been made. The treatment can be expected to be collision-free if the isocenter is within
the patient protection zone.
In planning, the patient protection zone is 5.0 mm smaller in every direction than in treatment. This
ensures that the planned isocenter position allows the couch to be inside the patient protection zone in
treatment. The 5.0 mm margin compensates for the setup errors, caused, for example, by:
 Difference in patient position in the Encompass fixation device between the CT scan and the
treatment.
 Accuracy of the alignment of Encompass support structures against the patient image.
 Accuracy of the absolute couch position values.
The patient protection zone in planning is calculated based on the central marker in the Encompass
structures. Note the following:
 CT images for HyperArc plans should preferably have a slice thickness of 1.25 mm or smaller.
 The CT image should include the patient's shoulders and the top of the Encompass, so that all
markers embedded in the fixation device are included in the CT image. This will improve the
accuracy of dose calculation, virtual dry run, and the alignment of the Encompass support model
against the patient image.
 Accuracy of the Encompass support structures alignment does not affect the accuracy of the
patient positioning during the treatment delivery, because the patient is positioned using imaging.
However, an alignment error in support structures may cause the plan to fall out of the patient
protection zone on the treatment console, thus preventing automated treatment delivery.
 If Encompass Overlay has been used as the fixation device, it is important that the Overlay has
been positioned correctly against the couch top in longitudinal direction. Otherwise, the fields may
be outside of the patient protection zone in the longitudinal direction and the automated delivery is
thus prevented.

<!-- page 266 -->

Automatic Isocenter Placement
During HyperArc plan creation, the isocenter is placed automatically into the center of mass of the
selected targets.
If the isocenter is placed in the central patient protection zone, full HyperArc geometry can be applied. If
the isocenter is placed in either one of lateral patient protection zones, only one-half arc rotation with a
couch angle of 0 degrees is possible.
If the automatically calculated isocenter is placed outside of all the patient protection zones, the isocenter
is moved automatically to the central patient protection zone and a notification is given.
The virtual dry run is executed only for plans where the isocenter is inside the patient protection zone.
You can view the patient protection zone in 2D orthogonal views and in the 3D Model View in External
Beam Planning and Plan Evaluation.
To view the Patient Protection Zone:
1) Navigate to the Planning menu> HyperArc> Show Patient Protection Zone.

Check the Plan Target Coverage
You can use the Target Coverage tool for a HyperArc plan before optimization to see how well the plan
target is covered with the current plan geometry. This is useful, for example, if you have manually edited
the collimator rotation of a HyperArc plan.
To check Plan Target Coverage:
1) go to the Planning menu option and select: HyperArc> Target Coverage

1
1
1

<!-- page 274 -->

2) View the display of targets in the Model view with the yellow color depicting full coverage of the targets
and the blue color depicting no coverage.

Note: You can move the isocenter of the plan manually to another location, if necessary.
When moving the isocenter, verify the field size and the collimator angle setting for each arc
field, or optimize the collimator angles again. To do this, choose Planning > HyperArc >
Modify Plan, select the Optimize collimator rotation check box, and click Apply.

<!-- page 275 -->

HyperArc Optimization
Once the HyperArc Plan has been created, you may proceed to optimization either directly through the
plan creation dialog or go to the menu Planning > Optimization > Optimize.
The goal of optimizing a HyperArc plan is to create a plan with rapid dose fall off outside the targets
without the use of tuning structures (Ring structures to control bridging and fast dose fall off).
1) Optimization with the Photon Optimizer will automatically define the lower dose objectives for the targets.
The dose levels defined in HyperArc plan creation wizard for each target will be translated into lower
objectives for the optimization and will be used to calculate the defined target quality metrics.

1

<!-- page 276 -->

2) SRS NTO will automatically be turned on (see section below for more information)

3) The structure resolution for HyperArc and SRS plans is by default set to 1.25 mm. The structure
resolution of 5 mm is no longer available in the optimization

2
3

<!-- page 277 -->

4) Jaw Tracking is automatically turned on

5) ALDO (Automatic Lower Dose Objective) may be turned on by activating the icon from the Optimization
Tool bar (see section below for more information)

4
5

<!-- page 278 -->

6) Once the optimization begins, The Target Quality Metrics are displayed in the optimization window.

6

<!-- page 279 -->

Stereotactic Radiosurgery NTO (SRS NTO)
A new normal tissue objective, Stereotactic Radiosurgery NTO (SRS NTO), is automatically set for
HyperArc plans. Stereotactic Radiosurgery Normal Tissue Objective (SRS NTO) controls the dose falloff
and dose bridging between targets. It automatically recognizes spatial arrangements of targets for which
dose bridging is likely to occur and tries to prevent it from occurring at least at dose levels higher than
17% of the prescription. SRS NTO is set on by default for the HyperArc plans. It cannot be used at the
same time with the Target Autocrop tool. The default structure resolution with SRS NTO is 1.25 mm,
which is the finest structure resolution available in optimization and the MRDC (Multi Resolution Dose
Calculation). If you have two target structures very close to each other, it is advisable to leave at least
1.25 mm space between the targets or contour the targets as one structure. For more information, refer to
the Eclipse Photon and Electron Reference Guide and Eclipse Photon and Electron Algorithms Reference
Guide.
There are two styles of planning for cranial SRS. Some users will only use lower objectives on the targets
and accept the inhomogeneity in the target and higher hotspots in the target.
Other users are more concerned with homogeneity in the targets and will place upper dose constraints on
the targets.
The SRS NTO is developed so that it reacts to the style of planning:
 If no upper objectives are set on a target or if the tolerated level of target dose heterogeneity is
greater than 40%, SRS NTO imposes stringent requirements on the rate of dose falloff, which
leads to elevated levels of irradiation through the centers of the targets. To increase the
heterogeneity, lower the treatment percentage.
 If there's a preference for target dose homogeneity or if the tolerated level of target dose
heterogeneity is below 40% and increasingly close to 0%, SRS NTO imposes a, increasingly
shallow dose gradient around the target.
 SRS NTO determines the tolerance for target dose heterogeneity as the ratio of the dose value of
the lowest upper objective to that of the highest lower objective.

<!-- page 280 -->

Automatic Lower Dose Objective (ALDO)
Automatic lower dose objective (ALDO) automates the attainment of equal relative volumetric coverage of
98% among all targets with lower objectives at the target specific dose levels for HyperArc plans with
single or multiple targets. ALDO bundles the target DVHs so that the same coverage of the prescribed
target dose level is achieved for all the targets. ALDO strives to achieve 98% coverage, but if one or more
of the targets cannot achieve that goal, it will lower the goal to 97% for all targets.

When using ALDO, all target structures should have only a single lower objective at 100% volume.
The user-given weights and doses for target lower dose objectives are internally modified to push the
target coverages towards the desired value. The size of the correction in the weight and objective location
are bound, so it is recommended to set the priorities of the lower objectives to reasonable values, for
example to be similar to the SRS NTO priority. The defaults set by the system for target objectives and
SRS NTO work well.
If VMAT is restarted with the option “continue from previous” and ALDO is selected, target lower objective
locations and weights are set based on the dose distribution at restart. After the initialization, the objective
weights and locations are iteratively modified as described above. The VMAT optimization restarts at MR
level 4 if ALDO is enabled. This means that if you need to shift the objectives to account for shifts after
final dose calculation, turn off ALDO.  ALDO will always force you back to the same solution.
Note that as ALDO modifies the cost function during the optimization, it is to be expected that the total
cost values do not decrease monotonically although the optimizer is converging.
When ALDO is in use, the following restrictions apply:
 ALDO cannot be turned on and off during optimization.

<!-- page 281 -->

 Target lower objectives cannot be changed when ALDO is enabled. Other objectives can be
changed.

ALDO option is not available for optimization if:
 any target has more than one lower objective.
 any target has upper objectives.
 any target lower objective volume is not 100%.
 Target Autocrop or Trade-Off Exploration is in use
ALDO settings can be saved in the optimization objective templates. It is recommended to calculate the
intermediate dose in optimization if ALDO is in use.
For more information on ALDO: refer to Eclipse Photon and Electron Algorithms Reference Guide for
v15.5.
Plan Evaluation
Once a HyperArc Plan has been optimized and final dose calculated with AAA or Acuros XB, evaluate the
plan as usual.
AAA and Acuros XB calculation options have a new option to select grid size specifically for SRS and
HyperArc.

<!-- page 282 -->

Support of treatment percentage which is available in optimization is also available when viewing the
DVHs during plan evaluation.
DVH visualization is displayed below on the left in optimization where 18 Gy in 1 Fraction is prescribed to
90%. The right side displays the same plan in External Beam Planning during Plan validation.

Support of the Target Quality Indices which are available during optimization are also available after final
dose calculation in the dose statistics window.

Important Note: When planners go back into the HyperArc trajectory window through
HyperArc > Modify Plan and change one or more target dose levels, then the defined
dose prescription (equal to the highest dose to one of the targets) will not change
automatically, this needs to be checked manually by the planner if this is still correct

<!-- page 283 -->

HyperArc Plan Approval
During Plan approval, several checks and the virtual dry run will be executed in the background to
determine if all requirements are met to tag the created SRS HyperArc plan as an automatically
deliverable HyperArc plan.
Plan delivery status is indicated with an icon and a tooltip in the transversal image view, next to the
patient orientation indicator. The icon is also displayed in other 2D image views if the views are
maximized.
Plan delivery statuses are:
 A blue icon indicates that automated delivery is allowed

 A gray icon with a stop icon within it, indicates that automated delivery is not allowed

 A gray icon on its own indicates an imported HyperArc Plan where the Virtual Dry Run has not
been executed

Imported HyperArc Plans
To treatment approve an imported HyperArc plan, follow the steps below:
1) If the plan has 3rd Party Approval, unapprove the plan by Right Clicking on the plan and selecting
unapprove.
2) Navigate to Planning > HyperArc > Virtual Dry Run (this step is mandatory to treat approve a HyperArc
plan imported in from another system)
3) Once the Virtual Dry Run passes for automation, follow the usual steps to plan approve, schedule and
treatment approve a plan.
Remove Automation
You can convert a HyperArc plan to a regular plan by removing the automatic beam delivery support from
the plan. This may be necessary if the Virtual Dry Run fails and automation is not possible.
To remove automation:
Navigate to Planning > HyperArc > Remove Automation.
The field geometry setup is changed to isocentric, the technique is cleared and links from fields to the
fixation device is removed.

<!-- page 284 -->

Treatment Preparation
No absolute couch values can be defined. The couch values need to be empty unless they were acquired
during first treatment. If absolute couch values were acquired, they are displayed as read-only and will be
removed when creating a plan revision.
Once a HyperArc plan is planning Approved, it can be scheduled for treatment in the Treatment
Preparation workspace.
In treatment preparation, the technique of the hyperarc plan is visible in yellow in the top left corner.

Reference Points
When creating the HyperArc Plan, you select a reference point as your primary reference point that
should reflect the highest target dose.
Additionally, it may be desirable to track the dose to each target with each treatment.
1) Add additional secondary reference points without locations to track dose to the targets as they may have
differing prescription doses. You may do this in External Beam Planning or in Reference points tab. In this
instance, secondary reference points are created in External Beam Planning. Right Click on the
Reference Points Folder in the Focus window.

2) Click on New

3) Select a Target Volume from the Patient Volume list
4) Enter in the Total Dose, Daily Dose and Session Dose Limits
5) Click Ok

methods will track dose to the targets correctly.

Imaging Requirements
The HyperArc plan should always have a setup field with kV-CBCT planned. The setup field will always
be created by default when using the HyperArc planning workflow.
In scheduling, attach the kV-CBCT imaging to the setup field for every fraction.
Optional: MV-images can be taken between the treatment fields. Only MV-images are allowed (open port,
treatment port, double exposure, single exposure before and single exposure after). Treatment images
cannot be added at the treatment machine and must be scheduled in plan scheduling.
The scheduling for a fractionated plan with kV-CBCT on the setup field and MV images on the treatment
field looks like the image below.

Fields cannot be reordered. The field order is set by the HyperArc plan creation wizard to create a plan
that can be safely delivered with plan automation. If you try to reorder the fields, you will get an error
message as displayed below

Workbook Introduction
Overview
This workbook introduces the recommended Halcyon Planning workflow to the trained Eclipse and ARIA RTM
(Radiation Treatment Management) user.  It serves as an educational supplement to be used with the RDS
Treatment Planning Instructions for Use manual.  It contains lessons and topics supported by screenshots
that illustrate key steps.

equipment, software, or data.

<!-- page 6 -->

Introduction to Halcyon™
Overview
Halcyon™ is Varian’s newest accelerator platform designed to be a new standard of efficiency in
radiotherapy.  The unit provides a single pre-configured 6X FFF beam model, supporting RapidArc, IMRT,
Irregular Surface Compensator and Field-In-Field (FIF) workflows only.  It also introduces a new dual layer
rotating MLC (SX1 MLC) and MV CBCT.  To compliment this efficiency and simplicity of the Halcyon
treatment units (also referred to as RDS), an abridged workflow has been created to support all RDS planning
needs.
Eclipse contains the following new features for RDS planning:
❖ Supports RapidArc, IMRT, ISC and FIF workflows (dynamic MLCs only)
❖ Automatic insertion of the new non-rotating RDS couch structures
❖ Two-layered SX1 MLC is used in RDS treatment planning
❖ Bolus linking at the plan level
❖ Collision zone verification aids in isocenter placement
❖ Automatic addition of fixed MU MV imaging fields when creating RDS plans
❖ Automatic scheduling of RDS plans when plans are planning approved

<!-- page 7 -->

RDS Planning Workflow
Halcyon treatments require the planning CT image contain the RDS couch structures and imaging fields.  This
is necessary to improve the accuracy of dose calculation and account for daily imaging dose as part of the
total dose received by the patient.  Image acquisition and review is required prior to treatment delivery per
fraction.  Additionally, the unit captures integrated images of all treatment fields delivered.
To streamline the process of creating and scheduling all plan components related to Halcyon units, the
following workflow has been identified:
Note:  The following workflow assumes the patient’s CT images have been imported and registered with
supporting images as needed, tumor volumes and OARs have been contoured
1. In External Beam Planning, insert a new plan following the prompts in the plan creation wizard.
2. Select the halcyon machine

Figure 1:  Plan Creation Wizard - Machine Selection

1
2

<!-- page 8 -->

After choosing the RDS machine, a new dialogue appears for the selection of the imaging setup template to
be used for daily positioning.   Imaging is a required component of all RDS plans and always included in
optimization and dose calculation for the Halcyon units.  Each imaging template has hardcoded prescribed
MUs and appear as “imaging treatment fields” in the focus window.  The available templates are:
❖
MVCBCT High Quality:  10 MU/fx
❖
MVCBCT Low Dose:  5 MU/fx
❖
MV/MV HQ:  2 MU/image per fraction
❖
MV/MV LD:  1 MU/image per fraction

3.  Select the desired template, then click Next.

Figure 2:  Imaging Setup Dialogue

3

<!-- page 9 -->

The next dialogue will allow you to set the dimensions of your imaging setup fields.  As Halcyon, does not
have jaws, the “Fixed Field Size” is defined by the MLC opening.  MLCs are added to all field types.  With leaf
travel on the x-axis, defining the y-axis is all that is required for MV CBCT images.  Also available is the ability
to set the size of the imaging fields relative to the dimensions of the primary target volume.  Using this option,
a user can define a margin value around the target to create the imaging setup fields.
4. Select the desired Imaging Setup Parameters.
5. Click Next. Complete the plan creation process as directed in the wizard.

Figure 3:  Imaging Setup Parameters

Note: Only one imaging setup up template per plan is
supported with Halcyon units.

The system generates an un-editable setup field and a treatment field, both with the same open MLCs.

Figure 4:  Imaging Field and Icon

4
5
Note the setup
field icon

Editing Imaging Setup Fields
If you need to change the setup imaging template after creating your plan, a new option exists to do so.
1. RMC on the plan
2. Select New Imaging Setup
3. Select the appropriate template
4. Choose the imaging setup parameters then click Finish.

Figure 7:  Changing Imaging Setup
1
3
2
4

<!-- page 11 -->

Once your plan is created, verify the couch structures has been applied to the 3D image appropriately.  Edit
as necessary.  If an adjustment is necessary
1. Select a couch structure from the focus window
2. Select the Move All Support Structures icon from the tool bar

3. Adjust couch position, then deselect tool.

Figure 8:  Moving Couch Structures
The RDS couch cannot be rotated and is always 0 degrees.

1
2
3

<!-- page 12 -->

Next, verify the isocenter position.  RDS plans only support a single isocenter setup.  Any fields moved, shifts
all and the single shared isocenter is retained. To change the isocenter position:
1. From the focus window, select any of the plan fields
2. In a 2D view or in the BEV, place the mouse pointer over the field's isocenter and drag it to a new
position.
-OR-
3. The RMC option on a field still provides the ability to align to structure or viewing plane intersection.

Figure 9:  Verify/Edit Isocenter Position
Eclipse contains RDS specific collision checks that can prevent plan optimization, dose calculation and plan
approval. If there is a risk of collision, the bore of the RDS treatment unit is highlighted in red in the 2D views.
Also, the Arc Geometry Tool will display a warning in case of a possible collision.
The outer circle in the image views represents the physical bore, and the collision is checked against the body
structure. The inner circle represents the safety margin, which is checked against the couch.

Figure 10:  Bore Collision Detection
Physical Bore

2 cm from Physical Bore
2
1
3

<!-- page 13 -->

Once the isocenter has been positioned and you are satisfied with the imaging set up fields, proceed with the
plan creation using one of the 4 plan techniques supported:
❖
IMRT
❖
RapidArc
❖
Irregular Surface Compensator
❖
Field-In-Field Technique.

For information on the workflow utilizing any of the mentioned techniques and the new MLC characteristics,
refer to the RDS Treatment Planning Instructions for Use.
When editing the field geometry, keep in mind the characteristics of the SX1 MLCs of the Halcyon unit.
❖ Fixed primary collimator of 28x28 cm
▪
Max size only available with collimator angles of 0, 90, 270 degrees
❖ No jaws:  Fields are defined by MLC position within the primary collimator
❖ Leaf width is 1cm
❖ Aperture is defined by distal leaf bank (closest to patient)
❖ Proximal leaf bank provides a “per leaf” tracking (closest to source)
❖ Proximal and Distal leaf banks offset 0.5 cm of each other
❖ MLC have 100% over travel (no splitting of fields)

Figure 11:  MLC Characteristics

Figure 22:  Field Information Panel - No Entry for Field Size

Bolus with RDS Plans
Maintaining the efficiency with RDS planning, assigning bolus to RDS plans links the bolus on the plan level
rather than to individual fields.  When a bolus is linked to a plan, all MV imaging fields and treatment fields in
the plan are linked to the same bolus.  Multiple boluses may be associated to the structure set and left
unlinked.  You may also link more than one bolus to a plan.
Create bolus as previously.
1. Select New Bolus from the Insert menu
2. Move ROI handles to desired area.  Take caution with position as bolus will remain in place for entire
treatment.
3. Enter bolus thickness, click OK.

Figure 44:  Creating Bolus

collimator angle

4. RMC on the plan
5. Select Link Bolus …

DRR Handling with Halcyon Plans
The Halcyon machine provides 2D/3D imaging as a standard.  This is to maintain the simplicity and efficiency
characteristic of the RDS planning process.  DRRs are not required in the plan at all.  Any DRRs created in
the plan will not be assigned as a reference image and consequently will not appear at the treatment unit.
The recommended workflow is to eliminate the DRR creation in your planning process.

Figure 77:  DRR Handling

Note: If specific DRR parameters are desired, they can only be created in
Eclipse and must be assigned as the reference image in Plan Parameters.
The option to assign reference images in Eclipse is disabled for Halcyon
plans.

Plan Approvals
Maintaining the scope of simplicity, the Planning and Treatment approval process has been enhanced with
automated scheduling.  All approvals can be completed within Eclipse for most cases, providing all plan
parameters have been entered.  Ensure tolerance tables and dose limits have been entered for the plan.
Plan Approval still guides you through some plan checks.
1. Warnings & Errors review
2. Dose Summary review
3. Parameters Review
▪
Note the only item available in this dialogue is SSD verification
▪
Structure outlines are generated with 2D/3D images at the unit
▪
DRRs are generated with 2D/3D imaging at the unit
▪
Treatment Time is calculated at the unit

Figure 88:  RDS Plan Approval

Note: The Delta Couch Shift dialogue box may display during plan
approval. Delta Couch Shift is not supported with Halcyon.

<!-- page 18 -->

Upon Plan Approval, RDS automatically schedules all treatment fractions and imaging for all fields.  If updates
are made to the plan and it is reapproved, the following message appears:

Figure 19:  External Beam Planning – Updated Auto Scheduling

If existing plans are scheduled within the same course, the system gives you the option to replace the plan or
directs you the Plan Scheduling workspace to complete the scheduling.

Figure 20:  External Beam Planning – Auto Scheduling with Existing Plans

Treatment Approval is now available in External Beam Planning providing the auto scheduling was completed
and all reference points’ total and fractional doses have been entered in their properties. With this scenario,
there is no need to go to the RTM workspaces for plan preparation.

Halcyon RTM Plan Features
The defined workflow limits planning functionality across the RTM platform.  All RDS plans must be created in
Eclipse and have a 3D dose in Plan Parameters.  This requires all geometric edits to be performed in Eclipse
as the 3D dose will need to be recalculated.  Plan specific edits and preparation are limited to the following:
(*) Denotes editable in plans with an unapproved status
Plan Parameters
▪
Treatment Field Names*
▪
Tolerance Table*
▪
Field setup notes and photos
▪
Field ordering
▪
Create/edit secondary reference points*
▪
Image Processing (including set as reference image) *
▪
Plan and Treatment approvals

<!-- page 22 -->

RT Summary
▪
This workspace maintains full functionality with some additions.

Image-guided radiotherapy (IGRT) is a requirement for all RDS treatments and is included in the plan
calculation.  The dose accumulation feature is automatically enabled (hardcoded) for all Halcyon
plans.  As the plans include fractional doses per image, any additional imaging at the unit will exceed
the session doses and display as an overtreatment.

Figure 25:  RT Summary

<!-- page 23 -->

The Halcyon unit does not provide for entry of treatment notes at the console.  Consequently, the
information displayed in the notes section is generated by the unit and is specific to the setup imaging
completed for the session.

Figure 26:  RT Summary – Halcyon Treatment History Notes
